  type: core | feedback | user | reference
  level: core | context | generic      # default: generic
  enforcement: validated | reminder    # required only for level: core

Default to generic. Do not set level: core here — Core is a promotion, and it requires wiring an enforcement hook, which is gt-promote's job.

After writing, gt-work actively asks whether any finding applies beyond this project and should go to Knowledge/, global-memory/, or a new project.

Also updates the stage:, topology:, and domain: properties in README.md when the project's reality changes.

Not in decisions.md: anything you might change next session. An ADR you reverse next week teaches the vault to lie to you.

Three questions when it writes an ADR (0.18.1): does this replace an earlier decision (gt_adr.py allocate --supersedes N, which gt_adr.py lineage and /gt:gt-query --lineage follow)? Does it have a known expiry condition (--expires-when "<condition>" or --expires YYYY-MM-DD, which gt-lint's adr-expires brings back for review)? And, for a new memory note, which entities does it cover (entities: [...], which gt_entities.py and /gt:gt-query --entity look up)? Each is a question, not a gate: say none and nothing is written.

What runs after the writes land (0.18.1). Each of these was once a step gt-work asked you to remember at the end of a session — the step that gets skipped:

Step Command Setting What it does
Research digest gt_digest.py write --vault V --project S — Rewrites research-digest.md: one line per entry (heading plus its first line, cut at 120 characters) for the newest 20 ## sections, newest first, and up to 5 whose heading carries [pinned]. Through the queue; indexed in memory/MEMORY.md once; research.md itself is never touched. Its frontmatter records the hash of the research.md it was built from, which is how /gt:gt-open knows whether to trust it
Contradictions gt_memory_check.py --vault V --project S --work memory_contradiction_check (on) Compares only the notes written or changed this session with their close neighbours: the same key: with a different value, or one subject said with opposite polarity ("disabled" against "enabled"). Each pair: both paths, both sentences, whether either links the other, and "same fact? which is current?". Nothing is modified
Promotion candidates gt_promote_detect.py --vault V --project S --work promotion_candidates (on), promotion_overlap (80) A memory note changed in 3 of the last 5 commits to its project's memory/ (→ research.md); research.md sections in two projects whose words overlap by promotion_overlap% of the smaller (→ Knowledge/); and gt-lint's global-scope-leak (→ demote). Each with a one-line reason; nothing is promoted without your yes

To pin a finding so the digest always carries it, add [pinned] to its ## heading in research.md — the one edit to an existing entry this allows.

The skeptic pass (skeptic_pass, on by default since 0.21.0). With it on, gt-work spawns a zero-context skeptic over the research entries about to land, before they land. Since 0.18.1 it needs nothing else: in 0.17.x it ran only when agent_specialization was also on, so turning the skeptic on also handed ingest and validation to specialist agents.

skeptic_pass agent_specialization gt-work skeptic ingest / validate
off off none inline
on off spawn inline
off on none specialist
on on spawn specialist

Learn: patterns worth keeping (0.18.1). Last, gt-work looks back over the session for patterns applied more than once, decisions made but not written down, and techniques worth reusing. It skips anything already captured and offers the rest one at a time, each routed to an ADR, a runbook step, a memory note or /gt:gt-promote — through the queue, never a direct edit. (A separate gt-learn skill was proposed; the owner folded it in here so findings have one path.)

Link suggestions after a Knowledge write (0.18.1). CONVENTIONS calls cross-domain links the most valuable and the most often missed, and a session that writes a Knowledge page rarely knows which pages elsewhere should link to it. After gt-work writes one, it lists up to five pages that probably should — cross-domain first, then by shared tags (3 points each) and shared title words (1 each) — each with its reason and a link type (cross-domain, hub, upstream, downstream, sibling). Pick by number; each pick adds - [[Target]] to this page's ## Related and - [[This page]] to the target's, through the queue. Skip, and nothing is written. Only the frontmatter of the other pages is read. A page's domain is its domain:, else the index.md heading it is listed under, else its first tag.

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_link_suggest.py suggest --vault V --page Knowledge/P.md [--limit 5] [--json]
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_link_suggest.py apply   --vault V --page Knowledge/P.md --to TARGET [...] [--forward-only] [--dry-run] [--json]

Exit 0 ok, including no candidates · 1 a write was refused · 2 usage. Matching is lexical — no synonyms — and /gt:gt-promote does not run the pass yet.

It offers a handoff for what did not reach a file (0.17.1). Classifying findings tells gt-work not only what to write but what it is not writing — a decision that needs you, something mid-flight, a question raised and unanswered. Those live in the conversation, and the conversation is about to end. So it names them and asks whether to write a handoff; on yes it runs gt_handoff.py and writes the narrative itself, because the script deliberately will not.

On yes it also raises the work, one task per unresolved item in the project's ## Tasks, each naming its own item and citing the handoff:

- [ ] Decide whether the sweep should block on cannot-run — context in handoff-2026-09-28.md [p:: 1] [waiting:: user] [since:: 2026-09-28]

A handoff nobody is told to read is write-only, so the task is the mechanism and the file is only the context. p:: 1, never p:: 0 — there is no p:: 0; 0 exists at project level only, where it is defined as a tier nothing occupies permanently. since:: is what makes it escalate: the vault's stale-P1 escalation rule raises the project a level once the task is seven days old, so an unread handoff climbs on its own and a read one costs nothing. One task per item, never a single "review the handoff" line — that competes with real work while saying nothing, and hides every item after the first.

The question is asked only when the list is non-empty, and that restraint is the feature. An offer made every session is one that is always declined — gt_closeout demonstrated exactly that by asking the same question every session until the answer stopped being read. Nothing uncaptured, no question, one line saying so. And a no is final: nobody writes a handoff on your behalf after you have said those items are not worth a file.

The next session is told, without anyone asking (0.17.2). gt_surface.py runs at SessionStart and names every handoff not yet handled, every session, with its count of open items. A handoff counts as handled once every task citing it is checked off — so the task must name the handoff's filename, as in the line above. That filename is the join; a task that does not name it is invisible to both the count and the automatic close.

/gt:gt-create handoff

Until 0.18.1 this was /gt:gt-handoff; the old name was removed in 0.21.4.

The session that designs something is rarely the session that builds it. This writes down what the next one needs — and marks what it must not assume.

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_handoff.py --vault "<vault>" --project <slug> [--repo <code-path>]

Writes Projects/<slug>/handoff/<date>-handoff.md. --json returns the facts without writing a file; --force is required to replace an existing handoff, because a handoff is someone's record of a session and overwriting one silently loses it. Since 0.17.2 it opens with frontmatter — type: handoff, project, status: open, created — and status: open is what keeps it in front of people until someone deals with it.

The script does half the job on purpose. It gathers facts — the project's stated goal, its open tasks, its recent decisions, the observed state of the code repository, what is uncommitted, what the last commits were — each carrying where it came from, so the next session can go and check it. It deliberately does not write the design narrative. A script that invents "what we decided and why" produces a document that reads finished and is not, and the next session inherits false confidence instead of no confidence.

That section is yours, and four things belong in it:

  • What was decided, and what was rejected. The rejected options are what the next session will otherwise re-propose and rediscover the hard way. This is the most expensive thing to lose.
  • What is built versus designed. Be blunt: "designed, not written" and "written, not tested" are different states, and a file listing cannot tell them apart.
  • What to do first, and what would make it wrong.
  • What is uncertain. An open question written down is worth more than a confident guess.

Verification labels are not decoration. Every fact is labelled verified, unverified or unknown, per Core rule 10, and the script never claims verification: it says what it observed and marks the rest. If the handoff says the tests pass, say who ran them, when, and which ones — "the tests pass" becomes folklore the moment it is written down in something formal-looking. Anything you did not personally observe this session is unverified.

The document opens with a checklist of questions the script cannot answer — whether the tests pass, whether the decisions were overtaken later in the session, whether anything agreed verbally never reached a file. Answer them in the document, or say plainly that they are unanswered. The script says so itself when it finishes: the output is not a finished handoff, and one left with its placeholder intact should be reported as incomplete rather than read as a design.

What changed this session (0.18.1). "What is the current state" is not "what did this session do", and the next session should not have to diff the vault to find out. When a session registers, gt_session.py register records the vault's commit as start_commit: in the session file (kept when the registration is resumed or taken over). The handoff then gains ## What Changed This Session, from git diff --name-status <start>..HEAD, labelled self-verified: new memory notes with their descriptions, the ## headings appended to each research.md, new ADRs, design.md files touched, and Knowledge pages created or updated. A session that changed nothing says "No vault changes this session."; with no start commit the section is left out rather than guessed. It counts committed changes only, and the diff is vault-wide, so another session's commits in the same window appear too. --since-commit <sha> overrides the start; --session <id> names the session (default $CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID); --dry-run prints the handoff and writes nothing; --json carries vault_changes.

Log the run in log.md with work, and link the handoff from the project's README.md so the next session finds it without being told it exists.

Exit codes: 0 written · 2 usage (--project takes a slug, never a path) · 3 the project could not be read, the target exists without --force, or the write would land outside the vault.

/gt:gt-query

Reads index.md, follows wikilinks 2–3 hops into Knowledge/, falls back to grep, then project memory. Be specific. If a result is status: stale, verify it.

Decision lineage (0.18.1) — /gt:gt-query --lineage <topic>, or "what is the history of decisions about X?":

python3 <vault>/Projects/golden-thread/tools/gt_adr.py --vault "<vault>" lineage <project> "<topic>"

Every ADR in the project whose title or body mentions the topic, with its whole supersession chain followed both ways, oldest first: number, date, title, "→ Superseded by ADR-M because: …" (M's context), and the one still standing marked — current with its rejected alternatives. No match prints No decisions found for topic '<topic>'. Read-only; one project per query; a sub-project's bare slug works. A chain is only as good as its Supersedes fields — written by gt_adr.py allocate --supersedes N (repeatable, and refused for a number the project has no ADR for); a title like "(supersedes ADR-3)" also reads. An ADR that superseded another in prose only shows up as a chain of one.

Entity lookup (0.18.1) — /gt:gt-query --entity <name>, or "what do we know about X?":

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_entities.py --vault "<vault>" lookup <name> --project <slug>
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_entities.py --vault "<vault>" list --project <slug>

Only the memory notes whose entities: frontmatter contains <name> (case-insensitive substring), each with its MEMORY.md description and full text — otherwise No memory files tagged with entity '<name>', and the ordinary search carries on. list shows every entity the project's notes declare. gt never writes entity tags: you add them, and gt-work asks which entities a new note covers. Read-only; exit 2 for an unknown project.

Answers from your own engine (0.21.4)

Every page /gt:gt-query reads stays in the conversation, and most of what it reads is context that turns out not to matter. With vault_answers local-first the reading is done by a model on your own network instead, and the session is given the answer: a few sentences, the file and line each came from, and the quoted line itself. It suits the question anyone asks of their notes, whatever the job — "what did we decide about the pricing page", "where does the on-call runbook live", "who owns the vendor contract", "what is the status of the migration".

gt_settings.py set vault_answers local-first            # off is the default
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_vault_answer.py ask "<question>" --vault "<vault>" [--json]
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_vault_answer.py stats [--days 7]
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_vault_answer.py mode             # is it on, and why

It needs the gt-farm module and one local: yes chat-completions row in the service table (farm_services; see Farming to Your Own Hardware). With the setting on, the skill runs ask before it opens a page:

  1. Find, without a model. Pages are scored BM25-style on the question's words, with gt_keyword_recall.py's page list, stop words and stemmer: a word few pages use outweighs one every page uses, so a bulky log that repeats "port" and "engine" no longer buries the small page that holds "engine-a". A hit in a page's name, or in a Knowledge page's index.md line, counts extra; curated pages (Knowledge/, global-memory/) rank ahead of project files with the same score, as gt-query reads them first; three pages with one file name (source.md) are the most that crowd the top. A retracted, superseded or expired page is never sent, and one ranked in the top three gives the question back.
  2. Bound. Each page is cut into headed passages. A passage must hold one of the question's words itself; the page's name and its section heading count for half. The best are taken, by the weight of the rare words they hold, and only as many as the engine can hold: 75% of its per-slot context (n_ctx, asked from the engine's /props; 4096 when it will not say) less the prompt and the answer, at most --max-passage-tokens (8000). When several engines are listed, the one with the largest context that answers is used.
  3. Scan. The passages go through the intake scan: text written to a model, or a credential on a line about to be sent, stops the question here.
  4. Ask. One request, bound to {answer, citations[{path, heading, quote}], confidence, needs_review, not_in_vault}. Only a local: yes row is ever used, and never a public address.
  5. Check. A citation is kept only when its file was one of those sent and its quote is found in that file word for word (whitespace folded; an ellipsis at either end, or a pair of quotation marks or backticks around the whole quote, is ignored; an ellipsis inside is a gap and fails). The line and heading shown come from the file, not from the engine. The rest are dropped. The quotes are proved; the wording of the answer is the engine's, and the output says so.

When it hands the question back to Claude (exit 3, ESCALATE (<reason>), and the skill then carries on as it always did): no_farm, no_service_table, no_local_engine or no_engine (nothing local to ask), no_candidates, non_current_pages, context_too_small, intake_scan, engine_error, schema_failure, not_in_vault (the passages sent did not hold the answer — Claude still looks, with the index and the links), needs_review, low_confidence (below --min-confidence, default medium) and no_verified_citation. Nothing is wrong when it escalates; it is the design.

A stopped engine is offered, never started. gt never starts an engine by itself, from a hook, or in a session that cannot ask. When no local engine answers and none is set up to start here, the result is no_engine and Claude answers from the vault. When a local: yes engine on this machine has a known start command — its gt-farm engine service record (gt_farm_engine.py install: a user LaunchAgent or systemd user unit whose file is still there), or one executable path in the service table's optional start column for an engine on 127.0.0.1/localhost — the result is engine_stopped with a START <engine>: <command> line. gt-query then asks you once per session: Start it now (Claude runs that command, waits at most 180 s with gt_vault_answer.py wait --service NAME --wait 180, and asks the engine once more), Not now, or Don't ask again (vault_answers_start never). The script itself cannot start a process.

The balance. Each question writes one vault-answer line to the savings ledger, estimated: what the session was shown against the cited files it would otherwise have read (each capped at 6000 tokens; a long log is read in a window, not whole). An escalation is a line with nothing saved and the notice's few tokens as a cost, so a setup that mostly escalates reads as the loss it is. stats gives the last 7 days: questions, answered locally, the hit rate, tokens kept out, what the escalations cost, and why they escalated. /gt-usage:gt-usage-savings shows the same row. The cost the ledger cannot see is wall time: reading is done by your engine at its own speed, so an answer takes seconds, not milliseconds. --dry-run finds and scans the passages and says what would be sent, which is the way to try a new engine; --keep-raw DIR saves the engine's raw reply outside the vault.

Read-only throughout: it never writes the vault, and a question's text and the passages stay on your network. Under gt sandbox mode the skill uses the gt-vault tool vault_report {report: local_answer, question}. It ships off; an unset value follows farm_first when that setting is yes.

/gt:gt-ingest

Bulk-imports an existing project. Copies — never moves or deletes.

The trap: if the notes cross-reference each other by [[wikilink]], renaming or merging files destroys that graph, because Obsidian resolves links by filename. Measure first:

grep -roh '\[\[[^]]*\]\]' --include='*.md' . | wc -l

Prefer keeping filenames. If you must repoint, match the whole link — [[name]] and [[name|alias]] — never a substring, or [[foo]] will corrupt [[foo_bar]]. Never repoint links inside Sources/.

Ingest as a staged pipeline (0.18.1)

An ingest runs as six stages, each reading only the previous stage's packet in <vault>/Projects/golden-thread/spool/pipeline/<run>/ — never a session's memory — so each stage is stateless and can go to a fresh agent, and per-unit stages run side by side:

Stage Runs as Parallel
intake-scan gt_ingest_pipeline.py survey (gt_intake_scan.py) per unit
survey the same command; units = top-level folders one
extract agent, spec extract-<kind> per unit
classify agent, spec classify-<kind> (optional) per batch
reconcile gt_ingest_pipeline.py reconcile + a reconcile-<kind> agent one
draft gt_ingest_pipeline.py draft, through the write broker per target file

The kind — code, docs, tool, session, wiki — is a parameter: one spec per stage in templates/agent-specs/stages/, a small delta per kind in kinds/ that may add prompt lines and fields but never change the executor, the intake-scan requirement or the context strategy. agent_specialization decides whether the agent stages go to specialist agents. It stops for you in three cases only: a direct contradiction with a fact already in gt (found at reconcile), a security issue (a credential, injected instructions, a unit the scan could not read), or unsafe source code. A stop names the kind of problem and where, never the content; draft refuses while it stands, and --owner-continue drafts everything else — the stopped findings are still never written. With no stop the ingest finishes with no prompt at all.

Units. Each top-level folder is one unit and root files are the unit .. Split a monorepo with --deeper packages or --unit-depth 2; --record --why "<reason>" saves the split in <vault>/Projects/golden-thread/ingest-units.json (in the vault on purpose: a config file inside the ingested repo would be untrusted material).

The workflow route (0.20.1). On a Claude Code with workflows (the Workflow tool, 2.1.154+, not turned off), a stage with many units can run as gt's gt:pipeline-stage workflow instead of one Agent call per unit. The deterministic half stays in gt:

gt_ingest_pipeline.py workflow-args <run> --stage extract --json     # every clean unit, scanned, rendered
gt_ingest_pipeline.py workflow-args <run> --stage verify --prompt c01=<rendered file> … --json
gt_ingest_pipeline.py packets <run> --stage extract --results-file <the workflow's result>

workflow-args writes each unit's prompt into the run's spool and prints the workflow's args: the stage's output schema as JSON Schema, and per unit still without a packet, the prompt file, its sha256, and the agent type (or model and effort) to run it at.

  • Extract renders its own prompts, each through the intake scan of that unit; a unit that fails the re-scan is REFUSED and never handed out.
  • Every other stage takes the prompts the skill rendered, and refuses a file that is not a render output for that job.

The workflow gives each agent only the prompt file's path, so nothing from the material reaches its task message, and Claude Code validates each agent's output against the schema as it returns. packets checks every result against the spec again and writes the packets.

A unit that came back empty is INCOMPLETE. Running workflow-args again hands out only the units still without a packet, so a stopped or failed run resumes. Inside one session, the workflow's own resume also works. A workflow cannot ask you anything, so every stop is handled after it returns, exactly as on the per-agent route. Without the Workflow tool, that per-agent route is the fallback and runs the same pipeline.

A writing agent's private scratch folder (0.20.1). The run's spool folder is per run, not per agent, and it is inside the vault -- the wrong place for extracted raw material, and a place sandbox mode denies every write to. So every unit of a stage whose agent can write gets its own folder outside the vault: ~/.gt-scratch/<run>/<stage>-<unit>/, every level created 0700 (owner only); on Windows %LOCALAPPDATA%\gt-scratch\<run>\<stage>-<unit>\, inheritance removed and only you granted access (icacls). The path goes into the agent's prompt, under Scratch folder, as the only place it may put intermediate files; only its JSON result comes back, through the packet, as before, and nothing reads the folder back. Whether a stage gets one is decided from its spec's tool list: a stage offered a tool that can write (a shell, Write, Edit or NotebookEdit -- one list, WRITE_TOOLS in gt_agent_spec.py) gets a folder; a read-only stage gets none, and neither its prompt nor its agent definition mentions one. Today only verify has such a tool (a shell); extract, classify, reconcile, draft, generalize and place are read-only, so an ingest run never has scratch. The folder bounds where verify may write; it adds no write tool to anyone.

gt_agent_spec.py render verify ... --scratch-run <run> --scratch-unit c01   # Agent-tool route
gt_ingest_pipeline.py workflow-args <run> --stage verify --json ...          # each item: scratch_dir
gt_ingest_pipeline.py status <run>                                           # shows it while present
gt_ingest_pipeline.py cleanup <run>                                          # an abandoned run
python3 <plugin>/scripts/gt_scratch.py check --vault <vault>                 # leaks: exit 1

A run's scratch is removed when the run finishes -- draft queued its writes, or promote-plan reached you with nothing waiting -- and by cleanup <run> for one you abandon. gt_scratch.py check (and the scratch row of /gt:gt-doctor) names any folder a finished or vanished run left behind; it never removes anything itself. Sandbox mode allows writes to ~/.gt-scratch (below).

--dry-run counts wherever it is placed (0.20.1). gt_ingest_pipeline.py --dry-run draft … used to write: the subcommand's own default overwrote the global flag, so a preview queued and drained a real entry. Now the flag means the same before or after the subcommand.

Promote runs the same way when there are several candidates: promote-scan, then one agent per candidate for verify (zero-context; refuted or unverifiable candidates are dropped), generalize and place, then promote-plan, which ends at awaiting-owner. No command applies a plan: promotions stay yours. /gt:gt-work is the session kind: its drafted write-back is scanned for credentials and injected text, split into segments, extracted, reconciled with the same stop rules — the skeptic is now the reconcile-session stage, still decided by skeptic_pass alone — and drafted through the broker. The 0.17.10 spec names stay as aliases for one release.

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_ingest_pipeline.py stages
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_ingest_pipeline.py survey <path> --kind K [--project P] [--run R] [--unit-depth N] [--deeper DIR] [--record --why W]
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_ingest_pipeline.py packet <run> --stage S --unit U --result-file F [--replace]
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_ingest_pipeline.py fan-in <run> --stage extract|classify
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_ingest_pipeline.py reconcile <run> [--facts-out F]
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_ingest_pipeline.py draft <run> [--owner-continue] [--no-drain]
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_ingest_pipeline.py promote-scan --candidates-file F | promote-plan <run> | status <run>

Every subcommand takes --vault, --dry-run and --json. Exit 0 ok · 1 a stop for the owner (or refused) · 2 usage · 3 incomplete — a missing unit is never a pass.

/gt:gt-review

Empties INBOX.md: reads every unchecked line (and daily notes, if configured), asks where each belongs, files it as a task or project, and checks the inbox line off with a pointer. Regenerates the rollup at the end. A finished day's note has had its comments and empty placeholders removed (0.21.4, gt_daily.py finalize), but every open - [ ] you wrote under ## Noticed is kept, so the sweep finds the same items.

/gt:gt-sync

New in 0.18.1. The vault is a git repository so that what one machine learns reaches the others, but until now nothing in gt moved it: a session could open a project from files another machine had already superseded, write on top of them, and leave a divergence to untangle. Run /gt:gt-sync pull before starting work on a machine that has been away, and /gt:gt-sync push after /gt:gt-work; a bare /gt:gt-sync shows where the vault stands.

python3 ~/.claude/golden-thread/hooks/gt_sync.py status [--vault V] [--no-fetch] [--json]
python3 ~/.claude/golden-thread/hooks/gt_sync.py pull   [--vault V] [--dry-run] [--json]
python3 ~/.claude/golden-thread/hooks/gt_sync.py push   [--vault V] [--dry-run] [--json]
python3 ~/.claude/golden-thread/hooks/gt_sync.py behind [--vault V] [--mode cached|fetch]
  • status fetches (time-bounded), then reports commits ahead and behind, uncommitted changes and the age of the last fetch.
  • pull fetches, then git pull --ff-only. When both sides have commits it stops and shows the two counts before git does anything: gt never merges or rebases the vault; reconciling is yours. Uncommitted edits that touch incoming files are refused by git, and gt does not stash.
  • push runs the push check and refuses unless the vault is ahead of a real upstream, and refuses when origin has commits this machine lacks — pull first. It runs no credential scan of its own; the vault's pre-commit gate is that.
  • --dry-run fetches nothing and names the command it would run.

Every git call names the vault (git -C), never prompts, and has a timeout. To be told at session start that the vault is behind, set sync_check: cached compares with the refs on disk (no network; the line states their age), fetch runs one fetch bounded to 4 seconds. The line joins the existing push-check report. Exit 0 ok or nothing to do · 1 refused or git failed · 2 usage, no vault, or not a git repository.

Coding work

Two skills for the coding loop itself (0.18.1), needing nothing but this plugin: a plan you approve before any code is written, then that plan carried out one phase at a time, test first. Both run in the code repository; the plan file is not vault content.

/gt:gt-plan <task>

  1. Restate, and confirm. The requirement in three to six lines, then "Is that right?" — and it waits. A plan built on a misread requirement is the most expensive kind of wrong.
  2. Read what already decides things — the project's design.md, spec.md and relevant ADRs, and the code and tests the change will touch — and cite each in the plan.
  3. Risks and unknowns, each marked verified (read or run this session) or unverified.
  4. The plan: numbered phases, each with a goal, the failing test that proves it is needed, the change, and a done when; then a strict scope: block and an empty ## Deviations section (see The scope contract). Written, with status: draft, to the code repository's git directory (.git/gt/plan-current.md; gt_scope.py plan --path prints it) — since 0.21.4, no longer <repo>/.claude/gt-plan-current.md, which is still read when the new place has no plan. An existing plan for a different task is replaced only after asking.
  5. One question: "Approve this plan?" — and no code. Only an explicit yes approves, through gt_scope.py approve, which checks the scope block and writes status: approved, the time and a hash of the scope block; a new question, silence or "looks interesting" is not approval. Editing an approved plan's phases sets it back to draft: what was approved is what runs, and editing the scope block does it mechanically (the hash no longer matches).

/gt:gt-implement

Asks gt_scope.py plan where the plan is and refuses while it is missing or not approved (0.21.4: the plan is in the git directory, or at the old .claude/gt-plan-current.md). Then, one phase at a time:

Step What happens
RED the phase's failing test is written first, and seen to fail
GREEN the smallest change that makes it pass
REFACTOR tidied with the tests still green
Docs only if a documented interface changed
Checks gt_allin.py --only tests,scan, naming which tests ran
Scope gt_scope.py check --worktree: every changed file or new dependency outside the plan's contract, each with the plan's written reason or none (0.21.4)

Any failure — or any member that could not run — stops the run and says so. It asks before each next phase. It commits only through /gt:gt-allin-commit (a --dry-run first, then "Commit this?") and never pushes. At the end of the session, /gt:gt-work captures what was learned as usual.

The approval is recorded by gt_scope.py approve and read from the plan's frontmatter. The plan is in the git directory, so nothing can stage or commit it by accident and it leaves no untracked folder in the work tree; a plan from before 0.21.4 at .claude/gt-plan-current.md is still read, and is exactly what trips a release check that compares the work tree with the commit.

The scope contract (gt_scope.py)

New in 0.21.4. "Don't over-engineer" is a rule no hook can check, and a plan that names its files in prose is a reminder at best. The plan now carries a contract the commit step checks.

The plan holds a scope: block, strict on purpose, in a fenced block of its own:

scope:
  branch: feat/add-widget        # the branch this plan applies to
  allowed:                       # files the work may change (globs from the repo root)
    - src/widget/**
    - tests/test_widget.py
  generated:                     # files a tool rewrites as a side effect (optional)
    - dist/**
  new_dependencies: none         # or a list of package names

* stays inside a folder, ** crosses folders, a trailing / means everything below. Unknown keys, a missing branch, allowed or new_dependencies, an absolute or .. path, a tab and an unfilled <placeholder> are refused when the plan is approved. Allowed without being listed: the plan file, documentation (markdown, rst, a docs/ folder, README, changelog, licence), and a test whose name pairs with an allowed file (test_x.py, x_test.py, x_test.go, x.test.ts, x.spec.js, XTest.java for an allowed x). If you list a new dependency, list its manifest under allowed as well.

A second section, ## Deviations, starts empty. When the work needs something outside the contract, it gets one line: the file or glob in backticks (or dependency: name), a dash, why it was needed, and which simpler alternative was rejected and why. A deviation with such a line is justified; one without is unjustified. Whether the abstraction was necessary stays your call — the line is what lets you make it in the open.

gt_scope.py check compares a change with the contract: the staged files (the default, what a commit holds), everything uncommitted (--worktree), or the whole branch against its base (--base REF, or --base auto). Renames count once and pass when the new path is allowed; deletions count. It also reads the direct dependencies in package.json, requirements files, pyproject.toml, go.mod and Cargo.toml before and after, by name — a version bump is not a new dependency, and lockfiles and transitive dependencies are not compared (Python 3.9 has no tomllib, so the two TOML files are read by a small scanner in the script).

scope_gate What an unjustified deviation does
off nothing is checked
warn (default) it is printed; /gt:gt-allin-commit prints the same under --- scope --- and the commit goes ahead; a git commit typed in a Bash call gets the same report as context from the commit guard (below) and goes ahead
block check exits 1 and /gt:gt-allin-commit refuses, naming the fix — a line in ## Deviations, or take the change out. A git commit typed in a Bash call is denied by the commit guard with the same files and the same fix. --allow-findings does not accept it: the written reason is the reason the feature exists

Not applicable, and never refused: no plan, a draft, a plan with no scope: block (plans from before 0.21.4), a plan for another branch, a detached HEAD. A justified deviation is shown with its reason and never refused. /gt:gt-implement runs the check at the end of each phase and over the whole branch at the finish, so every deviation and its reason is in front of you before you say yes to a commit. Because the approval stamps a hash of the scope block, editing the block afterwards puts the plan back to status: draft (reverted: says when) and it needs approving again; adding a ## Deviations line does not. A scope_gate change is a security key: with gt unlock on it needs the fresh confirmation, like test_gate.

A git commit typed in a Bash call is checked too (0.21.4). /gt:gt-allin-commit and /gt:gt-implement run the check themselves, but a session can also simply run git commit, which goes around both. The PreToolUse guard guard_scope_contract.sh (installed by install.sh beside guard_foreign_checkout.sh; matcher Bash, so a Read or an Edit starts nothing) closes that. For a repository with an approved plan bound to the current branch it reads scope_gate and does what the table says: warn adds the report as context and lets the commit through, block denies a commit holding an unjustified deviation, naming each file or dependency and the fix. With no plan, a draft, a plan for another branch, a detached HEAD, a plan from before 0.21.4 or scope_gate off it says nothing at all.

  • It judges what the commit will hold, not what is staged when the command starts. git add a.py && git commit -m x is one call whose index is still empty when the hook runs, and git commit -am x stages nothing up front. So the guard copies the index to a throwaway file, replays the git add segments that come before the commit (and -a as git add -u) on the copy, and checks that. Your index, work tree and plan are not written; the git add it replays writes the same blobs the real one is about to. The target repository is the one the commit goes to — the cwd, git -C, or a cd in the same line.
  • What it will not guess at, it does not judge. A glob or brace in a git add operand (the shell and git match them differently), -p/-i, a commit that names paths or uses -o/-i/-p, a git verb that changes the index earlier in the line (restore --staged, reset, rm, mv, stash, checkout... — which is how a session repairs a refusal), a merge, cherry-pick, revert or rebase in progress, a command line it cannot analyse ($VAR, $(...), eval, sh -c), and files an earlier command in the same line creates. Every one errs towards a commit going unchecked, never towards a refusal that was not earned; /gt:gt-allin-commit still checks that commit when you use it.
  • It fails open, and says so when a plan exists. A git that hangs is given up on after 5 seconds (the hook's own timeout is 10); any error lets the command through. Only when the repository has a plan does it add a note: the scope contract check is DEGRADED and did not check this commit. With no plan there is nothing to degrade and it adds nothing. A commit in a repository with no plan costs one git rev-parse and two file checks; a command with no commit in it costs the interpreter start.
  • Its opt-outs are scope_gate off and a ## Deviations line, deliberately. GT_TEST_GATE=off, .gt-no-test-gate and test_gate off belong to the test gate and do not apply: its own refusal teaches a session to type the first, and a scope refusal that honoured it would be waived by every session that had ever skipped a test run. There is no environment switch for this one for the same reason --allow-findings does not accept a deviation: a word the session can type in front of the command is a word that switches off the contract it is held to.
  • Why a guard of its own, not a clause of guard_test_before_commit. That guard's early exits are right for the test gate and wrong here (test gate off, docs-only commit, no test command, a passing receipt), its escapes are the ones above, and its denials are recorded as uses of Core rule core_test_before_commit. This one is not a Core rule's mechanism and needs no vault.
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_scope.py plan --repo "<repo>"            # where the plan is, and its state
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_scope.py approve --repo "<repo>"         # after the user's yes
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_scope.py check --repo "<repo>"           # staged files against the contract
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_scope.py check --repo "<repo>" --base auto --json

Exit codes: 0 ok, not applicable, or warnings · 1 refused under block (check) or a plan that cannot be approved (approve) · 2 usage · 3 could not complete. It writes no vault and takes no --vault; --dry-run stops it putting an edited plan back to draft on disk.

Tasks and handoffs

Two kinds of "someone has to come back to this": a task (one line in a project README's ## Tasks) and a handoff (a file a session wrote because it could not capture everything). Since 0.17.2 both work the same way, and since 0.18.1 one skill per verb covers both, with the artifact as its argument:

verb task handoff project
create /gt:gt-create task <text> /gt:gt-create handoff /gt:gt-create project <slug>
open /gt:gt-open task <id> /gt:gt-open handoff [id] /gt:gt-open <slug>
list /gt:gt-list tasks [filter] /gt:gt-list handoffs —
handle /gt:gt-handle task [filter] /gt:gt-handle handoff —
complete /gt:gt-complete task <id> /gt:gt-complete handoff [id] /gt:gt-complete project <slug>

Bare /gt:gt-list summarises both lists; bare /gt:gt-handle asks which to work through when both have something waiting. Five rules hold for both artifacts:

  • Surfacing is the alarm; the task or handoff is the record. Session start tells you what is waiting; the README line or the handoff file is what gets changed when you deal with it.
  • Seeing is separate from handling. gt-list and gt-open only show; gt-handle and gt-complete change things.
  • Nothing is deleted. Done and dropped tasks are checked off with what settled them; a handled handoff keeps its file and gains a status line. Dropping needs a reason.
  • A deferral needs a date. It hides the item until then and brings it back on its own. "Later, some time" is a drop, said out loud.
  • Being told costs no context. What is shown at start is one line per item or a count — never a handoff's body, never a project opened.

The old names were removed in 0.21.4. Type the new form instead:

old (removed) new
/gt:gt-task /gt:gt-create task
/gt:gt-handoff /gt:gt-create handoff
/gt:gt-task-list /gt:gt-list tasks
/gt:gt-handoff-list /gt:gt-list handoffs
/gt:gt-task-handle /gt:gt-handle task
/gt:gt-handoff-handle /gt:gt-handle handoff

/gt:gt-create task

Create a task the way a developer drops a TODO into code: say it in your own words, and the skill writes one well-formed line into the project README's ## Tasks through the vault tool gt_task.py, so it ranks in TASKS.md exactly as a hand-written one would.

python3 <vault>/Projects/golden-thread/tools/gt_task.py add "<task>" --vault "<vault>" \
  (--project <slug> | --inbox) [--ref "<[[Page]] or vault path>"] [--p N] \
  [--waiting user|agent|external|parked] [--due YYYY-MM-DD] [--dry-run]

The skill decides what it can: the project this session is in (or asks once, offering --inbox for /gt:gt-review to route later), p:: 2 unless you say urgent (1) or someday (3) — never p:: 0, which does not exist for tasks — and waiting:: user unless the work is plainly the assistant's or someone else's. [since::] is stamped automatically.

A task can be tied to what it is about with [ref:: …] — a [[wiki page]] or a vault path (Sources/…, Projects/x/design.md). The ref must resolve; the tool refuses one that points at nothing and says what it looked for.

One store, one parser. Tasks stay in the README; gt_task.py is a writer and a reader for that section, not a second database, and it parses with gt_tasks.py's own rules. Hand-written tasks are unchanged and work with every command here. Core rule 1 holds (queue-first since 0.17.11): the tool never edits a README itself — it queues the write and drains at once, and a README another live session has claimed leaves the write queued for the next drain (exit 1, and it says so).

/gt:gt-list

Tasks — /gt:gt-list tasks [filter]. Read-only: open tasks, filtered, without loading any project's context. Most urgent first.

python3 <vault>/Projects/golden-thread/tools/gt_task.py list --vault "<vault>" [FILTER ...] [--json]

A task must match every filter given:

Filter Shows
<slug> one project (sub-project as parent/child), or inbox
p1 p2 p3 that priority
mine waiting:: user
overdue due:: before today
stale p:: 1 open for more than 7 days
deferred only tasks deferred to a later date — hidden otherwise
ref:<text> tasks whose ref:: contains <text>

Each line starts with an ID, slug:LINE:HASH — the README line number and six hex of the line's text. Shelved tasks (p:: 7+) do not show unless a filter asks for them.

Handoffs — /gt:gt-list handoffs. Read-only: the handoffs still waiting — open, or whose deferral date has come — one line each (path, project, age, open items), oldest first, across every project or one. --all shows every handoff with its state. No handoff body is read: listing must cost no project context, which is why listing and handling are separate verbs.

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_handoff_status.py list --vault "<vault>" [--project <slug>] [--all] [--json]

Both — bare /gt:gt-list prints counts and the top of each list: how many handoffs wait (and the oldest), how many p:: 1 tasks wait on you (overdue, stale), and up to five of them — then the commands for the full lists.

/gt:gt-handle

Tasks — /gt:gt-handle task [filter]. The way to clear a backlog: choose a set with the same filters (/gt:gt-handle task stale, … task my-project p1), then take the tasks one at a time. Context is loaded only when a decision needs it — the file a ref:: points at, or one named file, never a full /gt:gt-open.

Decision Command
done gt_close.py task <ID> --vault V [--reason "<what settled it>"] — gt_task.py done, then the TASKS.md rollup
drop gt_task.py drop <ID> --vault V --reason "<why it no longer matters>" — reason required
defer gt_task.py defer <ID> --vault V --until YYYY-MM-DD --reason "<why then>" — future date and reason required
move (0.18.1) gt_task.py move <ID> --vault V --to <slug> --reason "<why it belongs there>" — writes the line into the other project first, then checks this one off with moved to <slug>; interrupted half-way it leaves a duplicate, never a lost task
keep nothing; move on

Done and drop check the box and append — done <stamp>: … or — dropped <stamp>: … to the line. Defer adds [defer:: YYYY-MM-DD]: the task is hidden from list, from the TASKS.md ranking and from escalation until that date, then comes back on its own. An ID is refused if its line has changed since it was listed — an ID from a stale list can never close the wrong task; list again and use the new one. At the end the skill regenerates TASKS.md.

A dropped task reads as closed to everything downstream — gt_events, gt_daily — because its box is checked; the line itself records dropped: <reason>.

Handoffs — /gt:gt-handle handoff. Where a waiting handoff gets dealt with. It lists what is waiting as a menu and loads nothing until you pick one — then only that handoff and the ## Tasks lines citing its filename, not a full /gt:gt-open. Each open item is settled one of three ways:

  • Done — the task is checked off with a note of what settled it; a decision goes to decisions.md or research.md through the usual /gt:gt-work path.
  • Keep — the task stays open. It is already p:: 1 with a since:: date, so it escalates the project on its own after a week.
  • Drop — checked off with the reason. Dropping is a decision, recorded, never a deletion.

Since 0.18.1 an item can also be moved — its task goes to another project (gt_task.py move). Then the handoff's status is set:

# every item settled -- the same close /gt:gt-complete handoff runs; refused while a citing task is open
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_close.py handoff <handoff> --vault "<vault>" --reason "<one line>"
# not now -- the whole handoff comes back on that date
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_handoff_status.py mark <handoff> --vault "<vault>" --status deferred --until YYYY-MM-DD --reason "<why>"

Bare /gt:gt-handle counts both lists and asks which to work through when both have something waiting.

Four states, and what decides each:

State Meaning
open written by gt_handoff.py, or re-opened. Shown until handled or deferred
deferred put off to a date (--until, which must be in the future). Hidden until then; on that date it is open again. There is no deferral without a date — "later, some time" is a decision to drop it, so it is handled with that reason, said out loud
handled marked so by a person, or every task citing the handoff's filename is checked off — the tasks are the record, so closing the last one needs no second step
history written before 0.17.2 (no status:), over a week old, with no open task citing it — so an upgrade does not resurface every old handoff in the vault

A handoff with no status: counts as open while it is under a week old or any open task cites it. mark appends a line to the handoff's status log and refuses a file outside the vault. A handoff is never deleted — it is someone's record of a session; its status says it is dealt with.

Opening one handoff or task — /gt:gt-open

Read-only, and narrower than opening a project (0.18.1):

  • /gt:gt-open handoff [id] — with no id, a numbered menu of the waiting handoffs; with one (a path, a filename, or a unique part of one), that handoff. It reads that one handoff in full plus the ## Tasks lines citing its filename — nothing else — and summarises its status, what it says to do first, what it says must not be assumed, and its open items. Then it offers /gt:gt-handle handoff.
  • /gt:gt-open task <id> — the task's full line and every field (p, waiting, since, due, defer, ref), its age, and — only if it has a ref:: — what that one file holds. An id whose line has changed is reported as such, with the project's current list. Then it offers /gt:gt-complete task <id>, /gt:gt-handle task, or /gt:gt-open <slug>.

Completing things — /gt:gt-complete

/gt:gt-complete <project|task|handoff>. With no argument it proposes the project open in this session, and otherwise asks what to close — it never guesses.

A project. gt_close.py project <slug> lists what stands in the way: every open task in the project and its sub-projects (deferred ones included — a deferral comes back), every handoff still open or deferred, and how many research.md entries there are. It exits 1 while anything is undecided, and the close stops there. Each task is decided one at a time:

Decision Command
close — it is done gt_close.py task <ID> --vault V --reason "<what settled it>"
drop — it no longer matters gt_task.py drop <ID> --vault V --reason "<why>"
move — it belongs to another project gt_task.py move <ID> --vault V --to <slug> --reason "<why>"
keep — a record, never ranked again gt_task.py shelve <ID> --vault V --reason "<why kept>" (sets p:: 7)

Each handoff has its items walked as /gt:gt-handle handoff does, then is closed. A handoff cannot be kept in a project being archived: it would surface at every session start for ever. Then the skill offers what the project learned for graduation, one finding at a time, through /gt:gt-promote — promote, defer or drop; skipping loses nothing, because archiving is not deleting. Finally:

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_close.py project <slug> --vault "<vault>" --archive --reason "<one line>" --dry-run
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_close.py project <slug> --vault "<vault>" --archive --reason "<one line>"

Archived in place (owner decision, 2026-10-01): vault_init.py archive-project sets stage: archived and archived: <date>, adds an Archived banner, marks the project's row in Projects/README.md, and records an archive event. Nothing moves, every link still resolves, and the project leaves TASKS.md. --move, only on request, then relocates the folder to Archive/<slug>/ and re-points every Projects/<slug>/ path reference elsewhere through the write queue (an edit made in between is escalated, never overwritten); it refuses while any live session claims a file in the project. Files the queue does not write — log.md, decisions.md, Sources/, spools — keep the old path as history. A relocate event is recorded. After a move the decisions spool stays at spool/decisions/<slug>, so a later project reusing the slug continues its numbering.

A task. gt_close.py task <ID> --vault V [--reason R] — gt_task.py done, then the TASKS.md rollup, which records the task.done event.

A handoff. gt_close.py handoff <file> --vault V --reason R (the file relative to the vault, or absolute) marks it handled through the write queue and records a retire event whose note starts handoff.close. It refuses while any task citing the handoff is still open.

gt-complete asks to complete; the close-out probe (gt_closeout.py, see Closing a project) is what suggests a project may be finished.

What you see at session start

gt_surface.py runs at SessionStart and puts four things in front of the session. A clean start says nothing waiting in one line, because silence reads the same as a hook that never ran.

What How often Setting
MUST DO — rows of deadlines.md overdue (🔴 OVERDUE Nd) or due within 14 days (🟡); later rows counted, not listed every session, recomputed live surface
Handoffs not yet dealt with — one line each: path, project, age, open items; never the body every session until handled or deferred to a date handoff_surface: any (default) · project (only when /gt:gt-open opens that project) · manual (only in /gt:gt-handle handoff)
Tasks — one line: how many p:: 1 tasks wait on you, how many overdue, how many open over a week, and the commands to list and work them every session while any wait task_surface (on · off)
State files gt_state.py wrote before a compaction once each; right after a compaction the newest one's content is handed to the model in full surface

Why handoffs repeat. The first 0.17.2 cut showed a handoff once and went quiet, on the gt_closeout lesson that a question asked every session trains people to scroll past it. The owner ruled the same day that the opposite failure is worse: a handoff scrolled past on a busy morning is as good as never written. So an unhandled handoff comes back every session, and the cost is kept to one line. any is the default because under project a handoff waits until someone happens to open its project.

The task line is a count, never the tasks, and a deferred task is not counted until its date. It is counted by the vault's own gt_task.py, so a vault not yet refreshed to 0.17.2 (run /gt:gt-upgrade) shows no task line rather than a guess.

deadlines.md format

One vault-root file with one table, one row per outstanding dated item — a credential rotation, a decision with a deadline, a renewal:

| item | category | due | see |
|---|---|---|---|
| Rotate the relay credentials | rotation | 2026-10-03 | [[pending-rotations]] |

due is YYYY-MM-DD. Ages and countdowns are derived from the date at run time, never stored, so the block counts itself down and disappears when the last row is deleted. Adding a category is a row, not code. The detail — a rotation's ordered steps, a decision's comparison — stays in the file see points at, because a rotation's danger is its sequence and a one-line alarm cannot carry that. A row that does not parse is skipped and counted; the rest still report. A label shaped like a credential is withheld, never printed, because this output reaches both your terminal and the model's context. gt_surface.py must-do prints the block on its own.

Reminders outside a session — gt_reminder.py

New in 0.18.1. The MUST DO block reaches you only when a session starts; a credential rotation once sat 15 days overdue that way. The reminder tool sends the same overdue and near rows of deadlines.md through channels you choose. Each push channel is off by default:

Channel Setting Needs
session start surface (on) nothing — the MUST DO block
macOS notification reminder_macos nothing (it appears as Script Editor; its notifications must be allowed)
SMS or Discord through your notification relay reminder_relay = sms / discord ~/.claude/golden-thread/reminder/relay.json (URL, optional auth), mode 600, written by your secrets store
email (SMTP) reminder_email ~/.claude/golden-thread/reminder/email.json, mode 600, written by your secrets store

To set one up:

  1. Read its setup: gt_reminder.py setup macos|relay|email.
  2. Relay and email only: have your secrets store install the credentials file, mode 600. A looser file is refused before it is read, and no credential value ever appears in a setting, the vault, the log or any output.
  3. Turn it on: gt_settings.py set reminder_macos on (or reminder_relay sms|discord, reminder_email on).
  4. Prove it: gt_reminder.py check <channel> sends a real test message and says DELIVERED only on positive evidence (HTTP 2xx, SMTP accepted, osascript exit 0); anything else is "could not deliver", exit 1.
  5. Schedule it: gt_schedule.py install reminder --vault "<vault>" [--hour H --minute M] (daily 08:30). It refuses unless a mirror exists, a channel is on and every enabled channel is configured, then proves the job through launchd — which sends a real reminder if anything is due.

reminder_days (7) is the window; overdue items are always included. The job sends once a day while an item is overdue — that is the nag; delete the row when the work is done.

Why the job reads a mirror, not the vault. macOS refuses a launchd job access to ~/Library/CloudStorage: it can see the vault and read nothing, which is how the weekly lint died for three Mondays. So the job reads ~/.claude/golden-thread/reminder/deadlines.json (mode 600), refreshed by gt_reminder.py mirror --vault V (the schedule install runs it) and at session start by gt_surface whenever a push channel is on or a mirror exists. Countdowns are computed from the due dates when the reminder is sent, so an old mirror only misses rows added or deleted since; one over 7 days old says "dates as of …". Credential-shaped labels are withheld before they reach it. Full Disk Access for python (every script would get the whole disk) and moving the vault were rejected.

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_reminder.py status                 # each channel's setting and readiness, mirror age, job installed?
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_reminder.py setup CHANNEL          # macos | relay | email | session
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_reminder.py check CHANNEL [--json] # sends a real test
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_reminder.py mirror --vault V [--dry-run]
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_reminder.py run [--dry-run] [--json] [--check] [--scheduled]
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_reminder.py import-tsv --vault V --tsv FILE [--only TEXT ...] [--category C] [--dry-run]

import-tsv folds an old label<TAB>date list into deadlines.md through the write queue, so there stays one list. The relay payload is gt's own shape ({severity, source, host, route, text, detail, ts}) — route on route — or format: discord-webhook posts straight to a Discord webhook. Exit 0 ok or nothing due · 1 a channel could not deliver, or the preflight failed · 2 usage · 3 no mirror.

Context management

Every way to spend fewer tokens, with what you notice when each is on, is in the guide Spending Fewer Tokens (/gt:gt-help tokens opens it).

The lean preset, the launcher flag and the effort lever

New in 0.22.0. Three things that cut cost, each with how well it is known. None changes a default.

  • gt_settings.py set savings_preset lean sets two settings, expiry_guard.min_tokens 120000 and rules_injection auto, and nothing else (auto_compact stays as you set it; its default is warn). set savings_preset none puts back what each was before; show lists what lean changed. The 120000 is the break-even of cutting before the cache lapses (about 115 to 120K, estimated, 1,139 idle gaps in 565 sessions) and has not been run end to end; auto rules reminders are the compact form for sessions under 60,000 tokens (estimated). New in 0.22.1: the cut advisor stays silent while a cut happens by itself (auto_clear on, or auto_compact auto, a percent or a token count); gt_cut_advisor.py why says so. For one switch over all the savings settings, see usage_plan below.
  • The launcher flag --exclude-dynamic-system-prompt-sections is a Claude Code command-line flag, so it is something you type when you start claude, not a gt setting and not a hook. In the savings session's test (measured, small sample) it cut a session's cost by 46% and a chain of six sessions by 16%.
  • Effort low cut cost by 13% on Sonnet 5.5 and by nothing on Sonnet 4.6 (measured, small sample); a rules-and-hooks-only gt profile cut 17% against 9% for full gt on Sonnet 4.6 (n=3), and 2% on Opus 4.8, which is not validated. These are directions, not promises.

/gt:gt-minimize

New in 0.18.1. A session left idle past its prompt-cache lifetime rebuilds its whole prefix on the next turn, at a price set by how large the conversation was, not how long the break was: measured, the median resume rewrote 432.7K tokens where a fresh session starts near 44.5K. Compacting while the cache is warm is cheap; after it expires you pay the rebuild first. And /compact silently summarises away what the vault exists to keep. gt-minimize makes the cut safe enough to do — measure, triage, prune, cut, quickly, because the cache is a clock:

  1. Measure (read-only, gt_minimize.py): the billed context of the last turn — a measurement — and the peak since the last compaction; a breakdown labelled as an estimate (conversation, tool output, tool calls, injected context), with what the transcript does not account for reported as unattributed; and the cache state — warm with N minutes left against the TTL the last turn was written at, or expired.
  2. Triage: what would be lost by a cut and is worth more than the session — a platform fact to Knowledge/ through /gt:gt-promote, a project finding through /gt:gt-work, another project's thought as one line in INBOX.md. Everything else is dropped, and named, so you can object. Nothing is written until you confirm.
  3. Prune: the keepers are written through the write queue (Core rule 1).
  4. Cut: "run /compact or /clear now — the cache is warm for about N more minutes". The skill cannot run them; they are your commands.

It writes no in-flight note (owner decision, 2026-10-01). Work in flight, an open question, the next command — that is a handoff's job, and gt-minimize sends you to /gt:gt-create handoff first. Across many sessions, rather than this one, the cost is measured by /gt:gt-optimize --only session.

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_minimize.py [--session ID | --transcript FILE | --latest] [--json]

It finds the transcript from $CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID; with no way to tell which session it is in it refuses (exit 2) rather than guess, and --latest guesses and says so. Exit 0 measured · 2 usage · 4 could not run. Output never shows a session id longer than 8 characters or an absolute path.

Cutting a session on a schedule: /gt:gt-minimize compact and auto_compact

New in 0.21.4. Every turn re-sends the whole conversation, so the cheapest tokens are the ones a smaller session never sends: measured on the owner's account, 86% of output came from turns carrying more than 128K of context. Cutting once at the end of the day is late; cutting regularly is cheap. Two ways to do it, both through the path the expiry guard already proves (capture before cut), so a cut never costs the vault anything:

  • On request: /gt:gt-minimize compact. One step. Claude saves this session's findings (/gt:gt-work), writes the handoff (/gt:gt-create handoff), then gt_compact.py request --handoff <file> asks the open session to compact itself when the reply ends. The session shrinks in place and the prompt box holds Read the handoff at and pick up where we left off; press Enter. The request is refused unless the handoff file exists. It needs Claude Code 2.1.296 or newer, where the in-session mod learns its session id; on an older one (gt supports 2.1.292+) nothing cuts and Claude says to type /compact, the handoff already saved.
  • By default, a notice: auto_compact warn (0.22.0). At the end of a turn where the context has passed 150,000 tokens (held to 75% of the model's window when it is known), one line says the session is large and names /gt:gt-minimize compact or /compact. It never compacts and never touches the prompt box. Said once; again only after the context has fallen back under the line and climbed past it, and the context nudge stays quiet on the next prompt so the two never say it twice. off says nothing.
  • Automatically, once you opt in: auto_compact. gt_settings.py set auto_compact auto (or 50: auto, a percent from 30 to 90, or a token count 100000-400000 such as 150k; auto cuts at 150,000 tokens, held to 75% of the model's window when it is known). At the end of a turn where the context is at or past that size, gt has Claude save new findings and write the handoff, then compacts in place and says so in one line: Auto-compacted this session at 62% context (124K to 9K tokens). Handoff saved: ... Press Enter to load it. It waits (tries again next turn) while a background task the session started is still running, acts at most once per six turns, and cuts nothing if the save step cannot run or ends in an error. The cache-expiry guard acts only after an idle period and the gt-state threshold only writes a state file, so there is one owner of "compact now" at a turn end and a cut cannot fire twice. Terminal Claude Code only (Claudian, IDE extensions and claude -p do not run the mod). The busy check reads the background_tasks list in Claude Code's Stop payload (confirmed in 2.1.300); a Claude Code that does not send it gets no cut at all, and the mod says so once a session. gt_settings.py explain auto_compact and /gt:gt-doctor (context-reading) say the same.
  • What a cut carries and what kind it is: cut_handoff and cut_kind (0.22.0). Two separate choices, for the cut you opted in to and for /gt:gt-minimize compact alike (warn never cuts; its notice names what a cut would be, for example a clear with a handoff and facts):
cut_handoff Before the cut
none nothing: a plain cut, no handoff file, nothing put in the prompt box
handoff a handoff only (what the session was doing, the next steps, the files touched); nothing is saved to the vault
facts (default) the session's new findings saved to the vault through the write queue, and a handoff with its key facts: the cut as it was before these settings
cut_kind The cut
compact (default) compacted in place; the handoff is offered in the prompt box
clear the session is cleared and Read the handoff at and pick up where we left off is put in the fresh session's prompt box, above anything you had typed. The in-session mod runs /clear through Claude Code's command API; where that is missing or refused it says to type /clear and puts the line in the box after your clear. /gt:gt-minimize clear forces a clear for one cut.

cut_kind clear with cut_handoff none is refused when you set it and again at cut time: a clear that carries nothing over discards the session. The owner's cut-savings tests found a clear with a handoff and its facts saved the most in the most sets; not measured live, and the clear has run only against a fake Claude Code in the tests. A clear happens at the same threshold as auto_compact: the auto_clear policy below stays a shadow record until the log has proved it (on is refused before that).

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_compact.py request [--handoff FILE] [--kind compact|clear] [--cwd DIR] [--json]
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_compact.py status [--cwd DIR] [--json]

What gt adds to each turn (0.21.4)

What gt puts in front of the model is measured, not guessed. The figures are characters on a typical turn with default settings and a vault whose index matches the prompt; each cut is behind the setting that already controls it, and the information stays one command away.

Added text When Before Now Where it is kept
Core rules every turn unchanged unchanged core-rules/ (not touched here)
Next-step choices line (hints_as_choices) first turn, then every 10th turn or 30 minutes 375 each turn 273, about 27 per turn gt_hints.py choices; gt_turn_notes.py prints them every time
Reply-length line (reply_length brief/terse) same 119 each turn 72, about 7 per turn the setting
gt-state line only within 15 points of the threshold, or when due 55 each turn 0 below that gt_state.py check by hand
Vault hints (vault_hints) a matching prompt; a title is not repeated for 10 matching prompts 275 each match 142, and nothing for a repeat index.md; titles name Knowledge/<title>.md
Session start: handoffs, MUST DO each list names 3, then (+N more ...) every item 3 and a count /gt:gt-list handoffs, deadlines.md

Routine successful runs of the scripts Claude runs most are one line: gt_broker.py drain when every request applied (--verbose lists each), gt_write_queue.py (names the request, not the queue file; --verbose adds it) and a healthy gt_doctor.py (all clear; --verbose, --only or any warning prints the table). --json output is unchanged. Subagents that return a result carry an explicit budget: the JSON only, free-text fields at most 40 words, at most 20 list items.

Checking what gt writes, reads and installs (0.22.0)

Text the assistant is about to believe can carry instructions you cannot see. 0.22.0 adds checks that look for hidden characters and sentences aimed at an AI before text is written to your vault, before an installed skill or plugin is trusted, and before an MCP server's tool descriptions are taken at their word. They run on an island: a process with no network, no way to write to disk, no secrets in its environment, and only a pipe in and out. On macOS the island is sealed by the operating system (measured: no writes, no network, no home folder, no view of other processes, no system services). On Linux it is sealed by bwrap, and on Windows by an AppContainer in a Job Object, where this machine allows it (a kernel that refuses user namespaces, or a Windows Python installed for one user rather than in Program Files, does not). Where it cannot be sealed it runs in a clean environment and the result says so; the doctor's island row shows which you have. Nothing needs Docker. A check that cannot run says "not scanned"; it never says "clean".

What Setting Default What it does
Hostile text in vault writes write_scan off | warn | block warn Notes rule@line in the broker log; block refuses hidden characters; a phrase only warns
Claims in vault writes fact_check off | warn | block block String-matches script and setting names against what exists; block refuses a write whose name is a near miss of a real one (a typo). A [[link]] or path that fails is only noted, because a write may link forward; so are a wrong "current version", a test count that matches no recent receipt, and a quotation its named source does not hold. Cannot judge whether a sentence is true; what it cannot decide is unverified and never blocks
One page per subject subject_check off | warn | block block A new page whose subject: another page holds is refused, naming that page; supersedes: [[Old Page]] replaces it, the old page stays and is marked superseded_by
Procedures you repeat gt_procedure_drafts.py find / stage ID / list / approve SLUG --yes / reject SLUG on request find reports repeated 5-step command sequences (first two words each, no values); stage writes a skeleton draft skill into gt's state folder only; approve --yes is the one thing that copies it into ~/.claude/skills, after scanning it again for hidden characters (plain wording is your review, so read the draft first); nothing runs by itself
Dating findings capture_date on | off on An append to a project's research.md with no date gets a hidden <!-- captured: DATE TIME --> line first; a newer entry whose heading shares enough terms with an older one in the same file is named in the broker log as maybe_supersedes; recall shows the newest entry date of a log page. Nothing is refused. ADRs: gt_adr.py date-backfill [PROJECT \| --all] [--apply] dates old ones from git (read-only unless --apply)
Reading text you do not trust gt_sandbox_reader.py FILE on request A model with no tools, no MCP, no hooks and a failing sandbox reports sentences aimed at an AI; the answer is capped, scanned and labelled as data; nothing it says is an instruction
New or changed skills, plugins, CLAUDE.md addon_scan on | off; addon_trusted_sources; gt_addon_scan.py scan [--all] / status on, none Scans on first sight and on change, reports once, labels local / trusted source / community. SessionStart runs scan --hook; more than three new units in one start are left unstamped until scan --all (the doctor suggests it). Reads and reports; never edits, moves or disables one. A plugin is gt's own only when its name, its marketplace (golden-thread-plugin), its install folder inside gt's cache and its files against its MANIFEST.json all agree (agents/ by name only: the installer rewrites them); a plugin that claims a gt name and fails any of these is scanned and labelled "claims a gt name, unverified". Not covered by that check: the body of agents/*.md, skills/, commands/, help/, plugin.json, .mcp.json and dot files inside an own plugin. A FIFO, a device, a symlink, a file over 512 KB, a file it cannot open and everything past 300 files is counted as "not scanned" and the plugin is never stamped clean
Writes to skills, plugins and MCP config addon_writes on | off on A Write or Edit to ~/.claude/skills/, a project's .claude/skills/, ~/.claude/plugins/ (not gt's own), ~/.claude.json, .mcp.json or ~/.codex/ always shows the permission prompt, whatever the permission mode. A shell command writing the same file is not seen
What a command is about to install install_guard off | warn | block; gt_install_guard.py scan SPEC / scan --command "CMD" warn Before git clone, npx, pip or npm install, claude mcp add or a download piped to a shell runs, gt fetches the target into a private temp folder without running it and scans it. A serious finding asks (warn) or is refused (block); a clean scan prints nothing. trust network allows curl, wget and package installs only while this is on. A one-off exception is a /gt:gt-grant
MCP tool descriptions mcp_pin warn | off; gt_mcp_pin.py check / status warn Tells you once when a connected server is new, adds a tool or changes a description. Reads the tool definitions from Claude Code's own transcripts, so it reports at the next session start; gt's own servers are skipped
Skills of your own that went unused gt_skill_report.py report [--days N] on request Lists the skills in ~/.claude/skills and a project's .claude/skills that no transcript shows called in the last N days (default 60). Report only; plugin skills are never listed
Repeated refusals by a guard refusal_limit off | count | stop count stop tells Claude to stop and ask you after the third refusal of the same kind
What gt loaded, and global-memory size load_receipt on | off; global_memory_budget on, 12000 One start-up line for you only; warns over the soft budget, never refuses
Search over past sessions session_search off | on; gt_session_search.py search QUERY [--days N] [--project P] / catch-up / status / rebuild off A private owner-only index of what you and Claude said, credentials blanked first, kept in ~/.claude/golden-thread/state/session-search.db. Off reads no transcript

Also new, with no setting: hints say how old their page is ("updated 3d ago"); the Stop validator keeps a local record of each block with its turn number (gt_shadow.py violations); handoffs carry your last three asks, the files and commands the session touched, and where its transcript is; a rule can run in shadow mode and the doctor shows the seven-day count of what it would have blocked; the doctor lists local skills unused for 60 days (skills); and gt_procedure_drafts.py find lists command sequences you repeat. Each of these reports; none changes your files.

Keeping the main conversation small (0.21.4)

Everything in the main conversation is paid for twice: once to write it into the prompt cache (1.25 times its size) and again, at a tenth of that, on every later turn until a compaction. A 12,000-token paste costs about 15,000 token-equivalents when it arrives and about 1,200 more on each turn after it, so the cheapest tokens are the ones that never enter. 0.21.4 adds four ways to keep text out, one rule that keeps them honest, and a table in the savings report that shows whether each paid for itself. All four are off or unchanged by default; nothing here changes a session until you turn it on.

The balance rule, in plain words. Handing a read to a helper is not free. A subagent opens its own context (its system prompt, tools and instructions) before it reads a byte: about 18,000 tokens for an Explore agent, measured on 629 agent runs of the owner's own transcripts. So a helper pays only when what it reads is bigger than that; under it, reading here is cheaper. A model on your own machine costs Claude nothing, so it pays at any size. Every mechanism below follows this, and the savings report shows the net: what the mechanism kept out of the main context, minus what it spent on Claude.

What Setting Default What it keeps out
Core rules sent once rules_injection full | compact full about 340 tokens from every prompt after the first on a fresh vault (estimate; measured by an independent agent, 25 Oct 2026); compact falls back to the full text when there is no transcript
A long command output sent to a file spill_threshold_lines off | lines off the middle of any successful command's output over that many lines
How a spilled output is shortened spill_preview smart | ends smart with smart, a test run, log, grep, diff or JSON document is shortened by its kind; ends is the first and last lines only
A very large message held back paste_intake off | ask | auto, paste_intake_chars off, 20,000 (72,000 with no local engine) the whole message, until you choose how it is read
A big file read by the cheapest reader /gt:gt-digest <file>; break_even, break_even_tokens on, auto the file; only the answer comes back
A session that has grown long context_nudge off | 20-90 40 nothing at once; it says when to close the thread or /compact. It reads the gt-usage status line, which a default install does not set, so until you add it (gt_usage.py --setup-statusline) it never speaks

context_nudge shows one line when the session's context reaches that percent (default 40), again every 15 points (55, 70, 85) and never on every turn; it never blocks a prompt, and a /compact resets it. gt_settings.py set context_nudge off turns it off. It reads the same context figure as the state write, which comes from the usage module's status line, so without that status line nothing is said.

Core rules once (rules_injection)

inject_core_rules.sh adds the full text of the Core rules (about 1,743 bytes, about 436 tokens, an estimate at 4 bytes a token; measured by an independent agent on a clean macOS 26.6.2 VM, 25 Oct 2026) to every prompt, and every copy stays in the transcript. With compact the full text goes out on the first prompt of a session, on the first prompt after a compaction (the SessionStart compact run, or a compact boundary in the transcript newer than the last full copy) and every 20 prompts; every other prompt gets one line: Core rules unchanged (10 rules, hash 4f2a) — the full text is above; re-read if unsure., followed by rule 5 spelled out (Rule 5: begin this reply — including any line before the first tool call — with the timestamp above.): the 90-run dev/ctx_ab A/B found compact cost 0.969x full per correct result, but replies opening with the timestamp fell from 93% to 84% without that clause. "Above" has to be true, so the hook checks the transcript, which holds a row for every prompt that reached Claude: if it holds no copy of the full text (a prompt another hook blocked, such as a held paste, is erased and so is what it carried), or the newest copy carries a different hash than the rules have now (a rule edited in the vault, a parallel_max that moved), the full text goes out again. In compact mode the full text's header carries that hash. When the hook cannot tell (no session id, an unreadable marker or transcript) it sends the full text. A hand-run echo '{}' | inject_core_rules.sh always prints every rule.

This is not a switch on enforcement (ADR-9). The Stop validator (timestamp, secrets), the PreToolUse guards and the hook itself run every turn either way; only how often the reminder text is repeated changes. For a rule with a validator that makes no difference to whether it holds.
