5% of the five-hour allowance, 91% of the context. Triggering on the allowance meter would have fired at entirely the wrong moment and looked correct doing it, because both are percentages with plausible values. A test asserts the tool never reads rate_limits at all.

It fires at --margin (default 5) below the flag point for your usage_alert mode, once per crossing rather than on every turn, and is wired to UserPromptSubmit. PreCompact is the backstop, not the primary — it fires when compaction is already starting, so the write competes with the thing it exists to survive — and its file says which one wrote it, so nobody reads a backstop write as the early one having worked.

A missing or unreadable usage ledger is reported as "cannot tell", never as room to spare: the usage module may not be installed at all, and silence from a source that was never there must not read as a measurement saying everything is fine. Every failure path exits 0; a state write that broke a turn would be worse than the gap it closes.

What you see (0.18.1). Until 0.18.1 the result went to stdout, which for a hook reaches only the model — the same failure the SessionStart checks had until 0.9.6 — so you never knew a state file had been written, or that one had failed. Run as a hook (detected from the hook_event_name in the payload Claude Code pipes in; the registered commands are unchanged, so nothing needs re-installing) it now speaks through systemMessage:

When In your terminal
UserPromptSubmit, context crosses the threshold, state written gt-state: context 91% — session state written to <path>
UserPromptSubmit, the write fails gt-state: context 91% — session state could NOT be written (<error>)
UserPromptSubmit, below threshold, already written, or no usage module nothing — a line every turn trains people to ignore it
PreCompact always one line: written to <path>, or NOT written (<error>), or context unknown: <why>

The model keeps its own one line either way. "Cannot tell" stays out of your terminal on ordinary turns, because with no usage module it would be every turn.

Whose reading it is (0.18.1). The usage ledger is shared by every session on the machine, and gt_state used to take its newest ctx_pct whoever wrote it — which is why, on 2026-09-29, a fresh session's first prompt reported context 88% — already written: the previous session's figure. It now filters the ledger by the hook payload's session_id (the ledger keeps its first 8 characters). A session with no reading of its own yet is "cannot tell" — usually the first prompt, since the ledger records about once a minute — which is the correct answer. By hand, --session ID names one; with no session known, the newest reading is used as before.

gt_surface.py — what the last session left, shown to the next

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_surface.py check [--vault V] [--dry-run] [--json]   # what SessionStart runs (with --hook)
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_surface.py must-do [--vault V]                     # the MUST DO block alone
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_surface.py handoffs --project SLUG [--vault V]      # one project's waiting handoffs (/gt:gt-open)

By 0.17.1 gt could write everything a next session needs and put none of it in front of that session. gt_handoff.py wrote handoffs, gt_state.py wrote state files, /gt:gt-work raised tasks citing a handoff, the owner kept dated must-do items — and, measured on 2026-09-28, none of the SessionStart hooks read a handoff, a state file, TASKS.md or any p::. Two credential rotations sat 15 days overdue at the vault's top priority, worked around almost daily, because priority is a sort order, not an alarm: sixty tasks already sat at the top tier across ten projects, and one more alerts nobody.

Surfacing is the alarm; the task is the record. This hook is the alarm. It never creates, edits or closes a task, and it never writes to the vault.

What it shows, how often, and the settings that control each are under What you see at session start; the file format is deadlines.md format. Handoff state comes from gt_handoff_status.py (see /gt:gt-handle); the task line from the vault's gt_task.py count (see /gt:gt-create task).

State files are still "once each", and that needs memory: a machine-local "already shown" ledger at ~/.claude/golden-thread/surface/seen.json, the one thing this hook writes. --dry-run leaves it alone.

A clean run says so — nothing waiting, in one line — because silence reads the same as a hook that never ran. It is never fatal: any failure exits 0, since a SessionStart hook that broke startup would be worse than the gap it closes. Switch it off with gt_settings.py set surface off.

Why this is core and not the report card. The request that led here proposed a deadline check in the report-card module. An alarm must not depend on an optional module being installed, and the report card shows what the previous session computed at its end, where a countdown has to be computed live at every start.

Session identity — which machine wrote a claim

gt_session.py claims a file for a session, and the guard_session_claims hook refuses a write to a file another live session holds, while the broker holds a queued write to it (Core rule 1). "Live" is judged by the recorded pid — but a pid only means something on the machine that recorded it. So every reader first asks: was this written here?

Until 0.17.2 the answer was "the recorded host equals this hostname". A hostname is not an identity. On 2026-09-28 a laptop moved to a wired network, DHCP gave it a new name, and every claim written under the old one stopped being this machine's. Nothing failed: the pids simply stopped being judged, and each claim would lapse on the heartbeat clock while its session was still running — Core rule 1, disarmed by a DHCP lease, with nothing announced.

Identity is now a machine id: a uuid4 written once to ~/.claude/golden-thread/machine-id, created atomically on first use (two first runs cannot mint two ids), never overwritten, never synced between machines. Registrations record it as machine:; host: stays as a readable label only. The claim guard, gt_session.py and gt_workers.py all apply one rule (gt_paths.same_machine; the vault tool carries a pinned copy, because a vault tool cannot import from the plugin):

The record has… Verdict
a machine id, and so do we the ids decide. Same id under a new name is a rename — reported once, claims still hold
no machine id (written by gt ≤ 0.17.1) ours while its host label matches, exactly as before. If it does not match, nothing can prove whose it is: its heartbeat decides, and that is said. It adopts the id the next time its own session writes it
a machine id, and ours is unreadable the host label, as before — and the missing id is announced

Two OS logins on one Mac are two machines now. The id lives under each HOME, beside workers.jsonl, which is machine-local by the same design. Each login judges the other's sessions by heartbeat, the cross-machine rule, where before they shared a hostname and judged each other's pids.

A pid no longer vouches for another session. One Claude Code process hosts successive sessions, so an older session's file recording our own pid looked live for as long as we ran, and its claims never lapsed. When the recorded pid is the asker's and the session is not, the pid proves nothing about that session, and its heartbeat decides.

/gt-watch:gt-watch

Module watch (plugin gt-watch), installed by default; it was /gt:gt-watch before 0.15.0. bash install.sh --without watch removes it, including the crontab line it installed.

Watch any git repo you depend on, and hear about it when it changes in a way you said you care about — raised as a P0 when it ships a security fix.

/gt-watch:gt-watch add <git-url>   — create a watch (any git URL: GitHub, GitLab, self-hosted, file://)
/gt-watch:gt-watch list            — every watch and its last change
/gt-watch:gt-watch check           — fetch now instead of waiting for cron
/gt-watch:gt-watch show <slug>     — Claude reads the new commits and explains them
/gt-watch:gt-watch ack [<slug>]    — mark changes seen
/gt-watch:gt-watch remove <slug>   — stop watching

A watch is a note — Projects/golden-thread/watches/<slug>.md, editable in Obsidian's Properties panel. Its frontmatter says what to watch: url, track (commits, tags, releases), branch, watch_paths, current_version, your own p0_when patterns, and starred.

How it runs. A cron job (gt_watch.py install-cron --every 1h) fetches every watch without Claude, classifies each change, and queues it. At session start a hook reads the queue — P0s first, spelled out; review items one line each; routine changes as a count. It never fetches. Machine state lives in ~/.claude/golden-thread/watch/, outside the vault.

When it is a P0 — decided by rules, never by a model reading commit prose: a new security advisory for the repo; a new tag or release naming a CVE or GHSA id, or saying "security fix/release/update" or "vulnerability"; a commit message carrying a CVE or GHSA id; a change to SECURITY.md or your watch_paths that says the same; or anything matching your own p0_when. A new release, a major-version jump, a change under watch_paths, or "BREAKING CHANGE" is a review item. Ordinary words like "auth" or "security" in a commit message are not enough — a P0 that cries wolf stops being read.

The module is on by default, but it fetches and reports nothing until you switch it on: setting watch (off · report). add offers to switch it on and install the cron entry.

/gt-demo:gt-demo

Module demo (plugin gt-demo), installed by default. bash install.sh --without demo removes it; --with demo brings it back.

A guided tour of Golden Thread on PizzaBot 3000, a fictional pizza-ordering project: twenty core acts, plus one act for each installed module that ships one — wiki, watch and flow by default, and workflow, lotr, farm, usage, report-card and pane when they are installed. It runs in its own throwaway vault, so nothing it does can reach yours.

/gt-demo:gt-demo start    — build the demo vault and print the command that opens it
/gt-demo:gt-demo tour     — (in the demo session) run the tour; you only click Next
/gt-demo:gt-demo end      — every commit and file the tour produced
/gt-demo:gt-demo clean    — delete the demo vault and build a fresh one
/gt-demo:gt-demo remove   — delete the demo vault; `bash install.sh --without demo` removes the module
/gt-demo:gt-demo status   — is there a demo vault, and how old is it

Running it: start builds the vault at ~/.claude/golden-thread/demo-vault and prints cd <demo vault> && GT_VAULT=<demo vault> GT_WATCH=report GT_WATCH_STATE=<demo vault>/.demo/watch claude — the extra variables let the watch act run without touching this machine's watch settings or state. Run that in a new terminal and type /gt-demo:gt-demo tour. For each act Claude says one line to the audience, does the work with the real skill, says what just happened, and offers buttons — Next, Repeat this act, Skip ahead, End tour.

The core acts: Core rules enforced (a prepared reply carrying a key is blocked) · open a project · "what's next?" from the task rollup · capture a finding mid-session · route a stray idea · validate a wrong claim · lint finds a planted broken link · promote a finding to a Knowledge page (which fixes the link) · then what 0.21.4 added: fewer tokens per turn (one-line startup, compact Core rules) · long output and big pastes kept out (spill, paste intake, the digest's route) · compact before it costs you (context nudge, auto_compact and the handoff it writes) · notes answered locally (the silent fallback, and the engine_stopped question) · what your own engine is good for (farm_first) · check the path (rule 10, the path-anchoring lint) · right model and effort (the effort report) · and last, file the inbox, close the session, and show the receipt. They are plain text in templates/demo-pizzabot/tour.md. The 0.21.4 acts run gt_demo.sh show TOPIC, which prints each real gt command before its output and runs it with HOME set to the demo vault's own .demo/home, so no act changes your settings; nothing reaches the network, a credential or a running model. A feature that cannot honestly run offline (the compact itself, calibration in both directions, the digest's reader) is shown as its setting under a "DESCRIPTION, not a live run" banner.

Module acts come from each installed module's module.json → demo file and are placed before the closing act: wiki — ingest a source and query the wiki; watch — watch an upstream library and see its security release open as a P0; flow — render the tour's events as a redacted flow view. A module that is not installed has no act. A module act names its scripts as <module:NAME>, which the demo resolves with gt_demo.sh module-scripts NAME (exit 3: not installed, the act is skipped).

Why its own vault: a fuller tour writes to shared files (INBOX.md, TASKS.md, log.md, Knowledge/) that other sessions also write. Undoing that in a real vault means rewinding history everyone shares. In a throwaway vault, clean just rebuilds it. The demo never writes your vault-config.json: the demo session is pinned with GT_VAULT, which every skill and hook honors. clean and remove delete a directory only if it carries the demo's marker file.

/gt-flow:gt-flow

Module flow (plugin gt-flow), new in 0.15.0, installed by default.

Draws how knowledge moved through the vault over time, as one self-contained HTML file that opens offline — no network, no CDN. One lane per project, time left to right, each lane split into the ladder's levels (memory → research/decisions/design → Knowledge → global-memory → Core); an arrow joins the level an item left to the level it reached, so a promotion is an arrow climbing. It reads Projects/golden-thread/events.jsonl and never writes the vault.

FLOW=~/.claude/plugins/cache/golden-thread-plugin/gt-flow/0.17.11/scripts
python3 $FLOW/gt_flow.py render --vault <vault> [--out FILE|DIR] [--redact] \
    [--project <slug> ...] [--since YYYY-MM-DD] [--tasks]
  • --redact before the page leaves your own screen — an Artifact, a chat, a ticket, a screenshot. Every project, path, task id, domain and session becomes a short salted hash and notes are dropped; levels, kinds and counts stay. Hashes are at least 6 hex characters (gt-flow 0.18.1; they were 4, and two renders shared one about once in 1,024 runs). Inside one render, two names that truncate alike get a longer hash, so a page never shows two names under one hash. The salt is random per render and never written, so two redacted files cannot be joined. If the redaction self-check fails, nothing is written.
  • Task events (task.open, task.done) are hidden at first — on a real vault they are most of the stream. They are one click away in the Kinds filter; --tasks shows them from the start.
  • --project is repeatable and includes sub-projects; --since takes a date or an ISO-8601 instant.
  • Without --out the file lands in the current directory (a temp directory when that is inside the vault). An --out inside the vault is refused.

Exit codes: 0 written · 1 bad arguments, an invalid event file or an unknown schema version · 2 no events yet (run gt_events.py backfill --dry-run to see what history would be recovered) · 3 the filters matched nothing.

/gt-visualize:gt-visualize

Module visualize (plugin gt-visualize), new in gt-visualize 0.3.0, installed by default. Two modes: explain and render.

Draws a repository as an interactive 3D code city, in one self-contained HTML file that opens offline — three.js is inlined, nothing is fetched. Directories are districts, nested so a directory's plot sits on its parent's; files are buildings. Height is lines, footprint is size (log scale), colour is language — from gt's own filetype definitions, the ones gt-scan uses — or, toggled in the page, how often the file changed in the last --since days. Drag to orbit, scroll to zoom, hover for a file's details, click a district to fly to it, search to highlight paths. (For how knowledge moved through the vault, see /gt-flow:gt-flow.)

VIS=$(ls -d ~/.claude/plugins/cache/golden-thread-plugin/gt-visualize/*/scripts | sort -V | tail -1)
python3 $VIS/gt_visualize.py render [PATH] [--out FILE|DIR] [--redact] [--since DAYS] \
    [--no-churn] [--exclude GLOB ...] [--max-files N]

Explain how it works — the second mode, and usually the one to reach for first. Where the city shows where the code is, an explainer shows how the parts work together: a narrative column that scrolls beside a 3D stage, each scene showing some parts, putting one or two in focus and animating flows along the links between them — data, a rule pushed in, an answer coming back, or a request stopped at a gate. The skill writes the story (a JSON file of parts, links and scenes) from the project's source.md, design.md and the code itself; the script renders it:

python3 $VIS/gt_visualize.py explain story.json --check     # list every problem, write nothing
python3 $VIS/gt_visualize.py explain story.json [--out FILE|DIR]

A story with any problem is never rendered. The rules are fixed, not left to taste (gt-visualize 0.3.0): scene 1 is an establishing shot of the whole system and the last scene a recap of it; every middle scene shows at least 3 parts and focuses 1–2; every scene carries a one-line caption of what the picture shows; and the page itself keeps the camera at one viewing angle, framing everything a scene shows and never closer than 55% of the establishing shot. --check names the rule each problem breaks; the full table (S1–S10, F1–F6) is in the skill. Ask for it in a session — "explain how this codebase works", "architecture walkthrough" — and keep the story file with the project if you want the next render to start from it.

  • Code city (render), below.
  • Inside a git work tree the file list is git ls-files --cached --others --exclude-standard, so .gitignore is honoured; outside one the directory is walked (skipping .git/) and churn is off — the output line says so.
  • --redact before the page leaves your own screen. Every file and directory name becomes a salted hash, keeping the tree's shape and short extensions; if the redaction self-check finds a surviving name, nothing is written.
  • Above --max-files (default 20,000) the deepest directories collapse into single buildings, and the output line says at which depth.
  • Without --out the file lands in the current directory (a temp directory when that is inside the vault). An --out inside the vault is refused.
  • The three.js bundle is pinned by hash (scripts/vendor/VENDOR.json); a bundle that does not match is refused, never inlined.

Exit codes: 0 written · 1 bad arguments, an unreadable path, a refused --out or a bundle that fails its hash · 3 nothing to draw.

Guided walkthrough (gt-visualize 0.4.0). Four skills turn an explainer into a guided session, each reading the story only through the script, never inventing a value:

  • /gt-visualize:tour [focus] — scene by scene: before each scene a prediction pause asks what connects its parts, then the scene and its hop map are revealed. Progress is saved per project (Projects/<slug>/visualize/tour-state.json, marked private and not a metric); a later session resumes with a two-question recap.
  • /gt-visualize:whatis <part-id> — what one part does at its own level of detail, with step into / step out / step over / where.
  • /gt-visualize:trace <scene-or-part> — runs a scene forward hop by hop on a real test where one exists, otherwise a clearly labelled STATIC walkthrough; ends with a hop table and offers a scenario test, written only on your yes.
  • /gt-visualize:explain-back [--diff <ref>] — opt-in only: 3–5 questions about the story's seams, answers checked against the story.
python3 $VIS/gt_visualize.py story scene <story> <title|index>
python3 $VIS/gt_visualize.py story part <story> <id>
python3 $VIS/gt_visualize.py story questions <story> [--parts a,b]
python3 $VIS/gt_visualize.py tour-state get|set --project <slug> [--vault V] [--dry-run]

A story that fails --check is refused. tour-state set is refused (exit 5) when another live session has claimed the file.

Publish (gt-visualize 0.3.0) — a page is local until you ask to share it. publish runs a scrub gate (credential scan, IPv4 addresses, home-folder paths, your scrub terms), prints the plan — target, visibility, final URL — and publishes only when re-run with --yes, which the skill does after you agree. Targets: local (default), claude (a private claude.ai Artifact Claude publishes; updates keep the URL), github-pages (<repo>/<dir>/<slug>/index.html, pushed with your git login; always public) and gist (secret or public, with your gh login — GitHub shows a gist's HTML as source, not as a page). A visibility a target cannot enforce is refused. No credential is ever stored.

python3 $VIS/gt_visualize.py publish page.html [--target T] [--visibility V] [--check] [--yes]
python3 $VIS/gt_visualize.py targets set github-pages repo=<clone> base_url=<https://…> [dir=visualize]
python3 $VIS/gt_visualize.py targets set scrub_terms=<file>     # one term per line
python3 $VIS/gt_visualize.py publishes                          # everything published, to find or take down

Defaults come from two settings, visualize_publish (local/claude/github-pages/gist) and visualize_publish_visibility (private/link/public), changed with /gt:gt-settings.

0.17.10 — queued vault writes, specialist agents, release announcements

Queued vault writes (gt_write_queue.py / gt_broker.py). When several agents write to one vault at once, each can queue its write instead of making it. (Since 0.17.11 queueing is not optional: it is Core rule 1 — see Writing to the shared files.)

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_write_queue.py --vault "<vault>" --path Projects/<slug>/research.md \
    --op append|replace-section|create [--section "Findings"] --content "…"   # or --content-file F
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_broker.py drain --vault "<vault>" [--dry-run]
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_broker.py status --vault "<vault>"

Requests wait in Projects/golden-thread/spool/queue/; session start shows how many. drain applies them oldest first and exits — no daemon, no hook. Appends from any number of sessions merge and near-duplicates are dropped. Two sessions replacing one section differently, or a section that changed after a replace was requested, write nothing: every version is saved in spool/broker/conflicts/ and a #conflict P1 task points at it. A file another live session has claimed stays queued (Core rule 1). Generated and protected files (log.md, TASKS.md, decisions.md, Sources/, core-rules/) are refused; design.md and global-memory/ always go to the owner, recognised by identity rather than spelling (see Writing to the shared files). Decisions are logged to spool/broker/log-<date>.jsonl. gt-farm files its results this way, and a farmed result can only create a new file, never edit an existing note (ADR-8).

Specialist agents by job type (agent_specialization, on by default since 0.21.0). With it on, /gt:gt-ingest hands work to a specialist agent (ingest-code, ingest-docs, ingest-tool) and /gt:gt-validate gives each validator a validate spec that loads no vault context. With skeptic_pass also on, /gt:gt-work has a skeptic agent flag unverified or overclaimed figures in the drafted research entries before they land. Full results go to Projects/golden-thread/spool/agents/<job>/; the session sees a summary. A job with no spec runs inline with a notice.

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_agent_spec.py list
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_agent_spec.py validate <spec.json>
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_agent_spec.py resolve --skill gt-ingest --path <dir>
python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_agent_spec.py render validate --template

Shipped specs are in templates/agent-specs/; a valid spec in <vault>/Projects/golden-thread/packs/agent-specs/<job-type>.json replaces the shipped one. (The job types are due to become stage × kind specs — feature request 2026-09-30-ingest-promote-stage-pipeline.)

Ingest scans first, stops only when it must, and runs on Claude only. Before anything reads material for ingest, /gt:gt-ingest runs python3 <gt>/scripts/gt_intake_scan.py <project-dir>: credentials (gt_secrets), unsafe source code (a download piped into a shell, exec of fetched or decoded data, obfuscated blobs, destructive filesystem commands, install hooks that fetch and run) and prompt injection (text telling an AI to drop its instructions, fake role markers, hidden Unicode, instruction-bearing HTML comments). Results are per unit — each top-level folder, plus . for root files; --unit-depth 2 splits a monorepo, --unit U scans one, --json gives the full report. A finding is kind, file, line and rule id — the matched text is never printed. Exit 0 clean, 1 findings, 2 usage, 3 incomplete (a scan that could not run is not a pass; unreadable formats such as PDF make a unit incomplete). Ingest no longer asks for approval: it stops for the owner only on a contradiction with a fact already in the vault, a security finding, or unsafe code, and writes nothing for a stopped unit. No ingest stage may run through gt-farm or any non-Claude service — gt_agent_spec.py validate refuses such a spec, and gt_agent_spec.py render refuses to produce an ingest prompt unless that unit scans clean.

Release announcements (release_announce, off by default). dev/publish.sh's announced step warned when no Discussion named the new version, and announcing was the step that got skipped. off keeps the warning; draft writes the post to ~/.claude/golden-thread/announce-<version>.md; post creates it in the repo's Announcements category with gh. One post covers every release no Discussion names yet, built from the CHANGELOG. It is scrubbed first (IPv4 addresses, home paths, scrub terms, credentials); a hit, or a scrub that cannot run, posts nothing and leaves the draft. Without a working gh it falls back to a draft. It never announces a version twice and never fails a release.

What 0.21.0 adds

Written against gt 0.21.0. The headline: hooks now ship with the plugin; an install asks every choice, security first; the paranoid group and single-use action tokens; I/O brokers for disk and network; a token profile, a models-and-cost page and a calibration schedule; the gt button pane; and /gt:gt-help. Breaking: /gt:gt-complete and /gt:gt-complete swapped meaning (below).

Install: the questions, in order (#33)

bash install.sh --questions [--json] prints every install question and installs nothing. Security comes first, then the rest, each with plain-language options (who it is for, what Claude does without asking, what it still asks, an example, the trade-off, "Not sure?", how to change it later). Every answer is also a flag, or a key in --answers FILE (the JSON the listing prints):

# Question Flag Default
1 How much may Claude do without asking? (security level) --lockdown strict
2 Fence Claude's shell off the vault and gt's files? (sandbox mode) --sandbox-mode off\|on off
3 Lock gt's installed files? (paranoid) Not asked: the security level's top rung, paranoid, answers it; the flag still combines it with a lower level --paranoid off\|on off
4 Quality against token cost (token profile) --token-profile minimum\|standard\|maximum standard
5 Which model size for gt's skills? --model-profile average
6 Your role (persona) --persona none
7 Shape drafts and summaries for that role? --persona-aligned on\|off on
8 How long should replies be? --reply-length normal\|brief\|terse normal
9 Compact idle sessions while the cache is warm? --expiry-guard auto\|warn-only\|off set by the token profile
10 gt-usage's status line? --statusline no
11 ast-grep for code search? --ast-grep no
12 Add the gt button pane? --with pane / --without pane no (yes for plain-mode roles)
13 Open the guide? --open-guide yes at a terminal

Not at a terminal, a new install with a security question unanswered exits 5 before installing and names the flags to pass; --accept-defaults is the explicit opt-out for CI, and the summary lists each choice as yours, chosen for you, or NOT applied. An upgrade keeps your current values. Through Claude, /gt:gt-init asks each choice with a question screen (security first), shows one review screen and then installs; on exit 5 it asks and never retries with the defaults.

The two hook routes (#12)

Hooks ship with the plugins: gt, gt-watch, gt-report-card, gt-usage and gt-pane each carry a hooks/hooks.json, generated from the hook registry (gt has 46 hook commands, including every 0.21 hook). Each entry dispatches through ~/.claude/golden-thread/wired/, a per-machine directory of the commands the installer wired, so a plugin whose files are not wired runs nothing.

Route When Where the hooks are registered
plugin Claude Code 2.1.136 or newer (the default) each plugin's hooks/hooks.json; gt writes nothing into settings.json
settings older Claude Code, or a version gt cannot read ~/.claude/settings.json, by absolute path into ~/.claude/golden-thread/hooks/

install.sh --hook-mode plugin\|settings (or GT_HOOK_MODE) overrides. A hook is wired once under either route, never both. Upgrading from 0.20.5 moves gt's own settings.json hook entries out after a backup; entries that are yours stay. gt_doctor and gt_components.py read whichever route is in use.

Token profile and reply length (#39, #42, #43)

token_profile (minimum, standard, maximum, manual; standard was called calibrated before 0.22.0, and a stored calibrated still reads as standard) is one choice that sets seven settings together, shown at the top of /gt:gt-settings:

Profile model_profile reply_length skeptic_pass vault_hints expiry_guard ingest_specialists model_calibration
minimum average terse off off auto on off
standard (default) average, plus stored calibration verdicts brief on off auto on report
maximum very-high normal on on warn-only on report
manual gt sets nothing

Change any one of the seven and show reads manual (based on <profile>); change it back and the name returns. reply_length (normal, brief, terse) makes the per-turn hook add one line asking Claude to keep replies to that length (answer first; brief is about five lines unless asked); it works in the terminal and in Claudian. allowance_note (on) with allowance_note_threshold (90): above that percent of the 5-hour or weekly allowance, replies carry the last saved reading and its age, so an app with no status line still shows it. gt-usage 0.1.8 writes latest.json for this.

Models and cost, calibration, and its schedule (#13, #28, #44)

/gt:gt-settings models lists every agent type with its model, effort, why, 7-day runs and estimated cost, and its calibration verdict; models set|reset|profile|apply-calibration change them. /gt:gt-calibrate runs the calibration pass now (an alias for /gt:gt-optimize --only calibration; it runs even with model_calibration off; the budget, weekly-allowance and terminal guards still apply).

  • One model record table (0.22.0). What gt knows about a model is a row of scripts/gt_model_prices.json models, keyed by provider and id: aliases, tier (fast, balanced, deep, or a word you add), input and output price, cache-read multiple, context window, supported efforts, long-prompt tier, a verified date and, for a model gt-auto-clear was measured on, its priors. A model id matches by exact id or declared alias, then by the provider's explicit, ordered prefix rules; never by a substring, so my-opus-proxy is not Opus. An id with no row is unpriced: the savings report counts it in an unpriced line instead of pricing it at a guess, and an effort cannot be set for it. To add a model that is not Claude, add a row (and, for a new provider, a providers block); tests/test_model_records.py pins that every Claude id priced before is priced the same.

  • The schedule. model_calibration_schedule: manual (default; only when you run it), weekly, monthly or off. The next automatic run is always counted from the last run of any kind, so a manual run today moves the next automatic one to a week (a month) from today. A run missed because the machine was off happens once at the next session start, never a backlog. The next due date shows in gt-settings and on the models page.

  • The cost estimate. gt_model_calibrate.py plan --estimate prices one pass from the kept prompts and prints it before anything is spent: for a home user (a subscription: a share of the weekly allowance) and a corporate user (API billing: dollars, per seat). --weekly-usd sets the dollar value of one weekly allowance; the default is an assumption and is printed with the result.
  • Budget presets. model_calibration_budget_preset: home (monthly, sample 2, a pass stops at the weekly-allowance line model_calibration_max_weekly_pct) or corporate (monthly, sample 3, a pass is refused once this month's spend plus the next pass would pass model_calibration_seat_cap_usd).
  • History. Kept prompts are held by age (pipeline_prompt_keep, default 28d, at most 200 per stage) and samples are spread across the window rather than the newest few. pipeline_keep_prompts (on) keeps them on every route.
  • Findings. findings_calibration also grades findings: no label above unverified without evidence, observed symptom split from inferred mechanism, re-labelled only on your yes.
  • Skills (0.21.4). The pass above grades pipeline stages because a stage keeps the prompt it was given. About forty skills run on a model and effort chosen by intent and never measured, and a skill keeps no prompt, so skill_runlog (off by default) records the evidence: a SessionStart catch-up (gt_skill_runlog.py) reads Claude Code's own transcripts, as the savings ledger does, and appends one record per gt skill run to ~/.claude/golden-thread/skill-runlog/runs.jsonl: the skill, the model and effort it ran at, tokens and wall clock, how it ended (completed, interrupted, error, abandoned, open) and a redacted digest of the task (the skill's arguments, and for a call the model made, the request that led to it; anything shaped like a credential is replaced). It reads transcripts rather than hooking the call because the transcript holds the whole run, a typed /gt:... and a Skill-tool call are different events to hook, and a session killed without a SessionEnd still contributes at the next start. Every digest passes the intake scan first; one that fails is left out, counted, never written and never sent to a model, and with the scan unable to run nothing is written. Nothing leaves the machine and the log is not written into a vault. With it on, /gt:gt-calibrate (or gt-optimize with model_calibration report) adds a skill source to the same pass: a skill with skill_runlog_min_runs completed runs (default 5; a skill's tasks differ more than a stage's, so more than the stage minimum of 3) is replayed on a sample of its digests (model_calibration_sample) one tier down, one up, one effort higher, one effort lower and at its current choice, with the skill's instructions and no tools, because the recorded answers had tools and could not be compared with a text-only replay. The zero-context adjudicator compares the answers and each skill gets keep, move-down, move-up, raise-effort or lower-effort with its agreement rate, the cost of each choice, the cost the real runs had, and the exact gt_model_policy.py set --skill <name> --model <m> [--effort <e>]. Nothing is applied. The skills join the stage plan, so one budget, the weekly-allowance line and the unlimited-budget confirmation cover both and a pass over budget refuses before any call and names what it skipped; report --skill NAME runs the pass for one skill to stay inside a small budget. A replay measures whether a neighbouring model understands and plans the task as well, not that it does the tool work as well. /gt:gt-settings models shows a Skills (measured) group: each skill's model and effort, its recorded runs, and its verdict and date, or "not calibrated" with how many of the runs it needs.
  • The cache-expiry guard (#27). expiry_guard (auto, warn-only, off) with expiry_guard.* (closed_sessions, open_sessions, handoff_pickup, resume_backstop, min_tokens 150K, margin_min, notify). The setting ships warn-only; the standard token profile sets it to auto, which compacts an idle closed session while its prompt cache is warm, or makes a compacted --fork-session copy of an open one.
  • Open sessions compact in place (0.21.2). A separate process cannot compact a session that is still open, so gt ships a Claude Code mod (hooks/guard_mod.js, inside gt's 2.1.292 floor) that runs inside it. With open_sessions compact (the new default) the session compacts itself about 12 minutes before a 1-hour cache lapses, saves the summary as a handoff, and says so; ask instead puts /compact in the prompt box so Enter does it (2.1.268 or newer); clear (0.22.0) has the session save a handoff and clear itself, and the fresh session is given the handoff with nothing to press (with handoff_pickup off the pickup line is put in the prompt box instead). With open_sessions fork, or when the mod cannot act, the guard makes the forked copy as before, saves its summary as a handoff, and when you come back the first choice is /clear: the fresh session in that terminal loads the handoff (handoff_pickup, on by default). Handoffs live owner-only in ~/.claude/golden-thread/expiry-guard/.
  • The guard arms at every turn end (0.21.2). It used to arm only on Claude Code's idle_prompt notification, a single 60-second timer that gives up for the whole idle period when you touched the terminal, a dialog was open, or background agents ran, so a large session could go unarmed for hours with nothing logged. A Stop hook now arms after every reply (the idle_prompt arm remains and only completes the pairing with the mod). If neither hook arms, the mod plans for itself at its last poll from vault-config.json, the context size Claude Code reports and CLAUDE_CODE_PROMPT_CACHE_TTL. Every case where the guard declined to arm is one arm-skipped line in guard.log with the reason and the hook (gt_expiry_guard.py status shows the tail); a repeat of the same reason is logged once.
  • Costs use the model the session mostly runs on. A skill on a small model can end a long Opus session; the rebuild estimate (the notification, the backstop) is priced at the most frequent model of the last 50 replies, not at the last reply's. The warm compaction still runs on the last reply's model, because that is the model whose cache is warm.
  • Cold resume starts small. When the cache has already lapsed, whoever compacts pays a full write at that model's price. The backstop therefore adds a choice, /model <small>, /compact, /model <main>, with both estimates, when a cheaper model's context window holds the session. A closed session the job reaches only after the cache lapsed (a laptop that slept) is compacted on the cheapest model that fits (gt_model_prices.json ladder and windows), moving up one model on a failure, only when the estimate is under $0.25 and closed_sessions is compact. Windows marked verified: false in that file are conservative guesses.
  • The ledger counts what a lapse cost. The backstop event keeps its actual_eq and counterfactual_eq (it claims no saving) and gains main_model, lost_usd, lost_eq, warm_usd, missed_usd and missed_eq: what the rebuild cost and what a warm handoff and compact would have saved, all estimated.

Lower effort, the effort report and the build-cost map (0.21.4)

Calibration used to look only one way on effort: it re-ran a sample at the next effort UP, so it could tell you to raise an effort and never to lower one, and lowering is the direction that saves tokens. The pass now also re-runs each stage and skill at one effort LOWER on the same model (a model with no effort, such as Haiku, or a choice already at the lowest level, has none). When that run is judged acceptable on every sample, with no schema failure and agreement at or above the move-down bar, the verdict is lower-effort, with the exact gt_model_policy.py set --agent <name> --model <m> --effort <e> (or --skill) to apply it. Nothing is applied. The effort runs need a runner that sets effort (the shipped claude -p runner does; the Agent tool takes a model but no effort): units that could not run are listed in the report under "effort units SKIPPED" with the reason, and units --agent-tool marks them needs-runner. The budget guard is unchanged, and the extra candidate is in the estimate.

Calibration proves a lower effort is SAFE for gt's own stages and skills. To see where the thinking tokens actually go across everything you run, including the main session and agents you spawn yourself:

python3 <plugin>/scripts/gt_effort_report.py effort [--days N] [--json]
python3 <plugin>/scripts/gt_effort_report.py builds --session-file <session>.jsonl [--gap SECONDS] [--json]

effort reads your local Claude Code transcripts (read-only, counts only, never a message) and prints, per task kind (a main session's first skill; a subagent's type plus a keyword class of its description), the model, the recorded effort, turns, input and cache tokens, output tokens, thinking tokens and the share of output that is thinking. Where a routine kind (a merge, a doc edit, a status check, a reader) spends 40% or more of its output thinking at a high effort, it suggests one effort lower: an agent definition with effort: in its frontmatter for an agent you spawn, /effort <level> or the effort setting for the main session. Figures read from a transcript are MEASURED; the saving is ESTIMATED.

builds is the cost map for a build with several agents. It groups a session's subagents into runs by launch time and prints, per agent, tokens, duration, tool calls and errors; per run, wall time, total tokens, peak concurrency, where the weighted cost goes (cache re-reads, cache writes, output) and the reading several agents duplicated (the same file by Read or a plain cat). It then ESTIMATES the same work run one agent after another (tokens about equal, wall time the sum) and as one agent in a single growing context (each turn re-reads everything before it, so it costs more and passes the context window). Read it as a model, not a measurement.

Persona-aligned output (#19, #45)

persona picks a role (product-manager, project-manager, developer, executive, leadership, operations, dba, network-admin); several combine in order. persona_aligned (on): each pack has an audience line, and gt_agent_spec appends an Audience block to the agents that write for you (draft, briefs, summaries, wrap-ups) and never to fact-finding ones (extract, verify, validate): role changes presentation, never what is recorded as true. Applied at run time; no reinstall.

Personas shape the session too (0.21.4, ADR-18). Each pack carries three more fields. featured_skills (five to eight gt commands) are offered first when gt suggests a next step (gt_persona.py featured). session_focus names the session-start sections shown first (must do, handoffs, tasks, write queue, sandbox, pickup, state); every other section follows in its usual order, so nothing is hidden. lotr_templates names the catalog templates gt-lotr suggests for the role in lotr setup with no template and in lotr template list. A persona changes what gt offers, never what it enforces; with no persona every output is exactly as before. /gt:gt-help has a guide per role (#role:<persona>) built from the packs, so it shows these three fields as they ship.

Personas shape the day's end (0.21.4). Two more pack fields say what /gt:gt-close puts first. close_focus lists items from a fixed vocabulary: what gt-work always captures (findings, decisions, design, runbook, memory, tasks, handoffs, promotions) and kinds of finding to look for first (code, deadlines, incidents, migrations, config, topology). close_summary: brief-first opens the wrap-up with at most five plain lines (what moved, what was decided, what is blocked) and keeps the detail below; executive and leadership set it. gt_persona.py close-plan prints the plan, and gt-close reads it before the write-back. A developer's close leads with files, commits and unpushed work; a project manager's with tasks, deadlines and handoffs; an operator's with incidents and runbook steps. Order and emphasis only: every gt-work step still runs, everything it would capture is captured, and the Core rules apply. Several personas: the union, the first listed leading. With no persona the plan is empty and gt-close runs exactly as before.

Handoff ids and closing a handoff (#22)

Code makes the handoff id H-YYYYMMDD-xxxx and each item a block id ^h1, ^h2, ..., cited by resolutions, tasks and the close output; older handoffs are back-filled. A handoff closes only when each open item is resolved: closed, made a task, or dropped with a reason.

I/O brokers: disk read, network fetch, disk write (#20)

Three tools on the gt-vault MCP server, each held by one agent that has nothing else. All three are gated (gate.io.read, gate.io.fetch, gate.io.write, always on). io_tools (on) offers the disk tools whatever vault_mcp says.

Tool Agent Rule
disk_read, disk_list disk-reader Free inside the repositories a project's source.md names; elsewhere presence (Touch ID, Hello or TOTP) or a timed window. Real path resolved first, 1 MB cap with paging, contents wrapped as untrusted data.
net_fetch net-fetcher GET only, no cookies or credentials. Domains in io_fetch_allow are served, other public domains ask presence; private, loopback, link-local and metadata addresses never. Every DNS answer checked, connection pinned, redirects re-checked (max 5), text types only, 1 MB cap.
disk_write disk-writer One text file inside a git work tree; presence on every write, or a live timed window for one repo or folder. Backup before overwrite, never executable, no commit, push or subprocess. Refused when no presence method is enrolled and Claude Code runs the tool without asking.

Never, even approved: ~/.claude, shell start-up files, LaunchAgents/Daemons, ~/.ssh, every .git component, .env / *.env and key files, .envrc, .gitattributes, .gitmodules, credential dotfiles, the vault, and CI workflow files (an owner-only lift, io_write_ci=allow). disk_read and net_fetch withhold credential-shaped text, naming the rule and line and never the value. Settings: io_read_allow, io_read_max_bytes, io_fetch_allow, io_fetch_max_bytes, io_write_presence (cannot be off), io_write_ci, io_write_max_bytes; gt_settings.py io show and io allow <folder>.

gt_settings.py io allow --preset public-data (0.22.0) adds www.sec.gov, data.sec.gov and www.federalregister.gov to io_fetch_allow in one write and changes no other fetch rule. A fetch from a sec.gov host carries a User-Agent with your contact details and is spaced to about ten requests a second: set GT_FETCH_CONTACT (an email, or a name and URL; plain characters) or the io_fetch_contact setting first, or the fetch is refused with contact_needed. The EDGAR policy is written from memory (unverified).

The paranoid group and single-use action tokens (#21, #32)

paranoid (off by default) protects gt's installed code: paranoid.os_lock (OS lock on hooks, packs, plugin cache and marketplace), paranoid.code_write_gate (a presence gate to unlock), paranoid.bash_guard (a PreToolUse(Bash) guard for writes to protected paths), paranoid.signed_manifest (each hook verifies a signed manifest of its files and fails closed), paranoid.plugin_dirs and paranoid.require_unlock. Each follows the master unless set; the status reads paranoid (partial) when a part is off (gt_integrity.py status, and a doctor integrity row).

paranoid.action_tokens adds a single-use token per action, with one on/off setting each: disk_read, disk_list, net_fetch, disk_write, lotr_read, lotr_write, lotr_consent, vault_read, vault_write, secrets_unseal, git_push, git_commit, publish, grant_add, plan_bound (each is paranoid.action_tokens.<action>, follow by default). You mint a token in a terminal with presence:

python3 ~/.claude/golden-thread/hooks/gt_action_token.py mint <action> <target>
python3 ~/.claude/golden-thread/hooks/gt_action_token.py mint-plan <plan.json>  # an approved plan: several tokens, one confirmation

A token is HMAC-signed, bound to the action, the exact target and the content, expires in minutes and is spent once by an atomic rename; replay, retarget, expiry and forgery are refused with the mint command as the route. Hooks read the token from the GT_ACTION_TOKEN environment variable (never stored); subcommands gate, git-commit-gate and fingerprint serve the hooks, and list, verify and status inspect them. The audit chain is keyed and holds no values. Exemption: gt-lotr's own daemon unsealing its own credentials is exempt from secrets_unseal.

The testing profile (#40)

gt_settings.py testing on|off|status|sweep (gt_testing_profile.py; setting testing_profile) pre-approves test runners, static scanners and gt's own dev test commands for one project (--scope user for all), with optional --sandbox edits, --until <date> expiry (swept at session start) and --dry-run. It records exactly what it added after a backup; off removes only that. It never writes modes, hooks, skip-permissions or GT_TEST_GATE, and every Core-rule gate is untouched. --scope local writes the project's personal settings.local.json instead; off with no scope ends this project's two files at once and prints the diff. When a run is blocked and you do not know why, gt_doctor.py testing names the gate and offers the one scoped fix, through this same record.

The gt pane (#41) and getting started (#36)

The optional module gt-pane 0.1.1 (Claude Code 2.1.290 or newer since gt 0.21.4, 2.1.296 in 0.21.2–0.21.3; install with --with pane) shows a 28-column pane of 12 bordered buttons (hotkeys 1-9, 0, q, w) that send plain-words prompts as your own words. /gt-pane-buttons toggles it; a small x closes it; Arrange mode reorders; Update Actions (below Arrange) or /gt-pane:gt-pane edits the list; Open Help (below Update Actions, key h) opens /gt:gt-help in plain words ("move Overdue to the top"). Your list is ~/.claude/golden-thread/pane/buttons.json, seeded once from your persona and never overwritten. Where the pane cannot dock (below), a button row above the prompt carries the buttons; an allowance footer reads gt-usage (pane_status). pane_at_start is open, closed or off. On an older Claude Code, or one whose version cannot be read, the installer neither asks about the pane nor installs it, and removes one an earlier install left; your recorded choice is kept, so re-running the installer after updating Claude Code puts the pane back.

The side column docks only in Claude Code's fullscreen renderer. Under the classic renderer (the main-screen layout: tmux by default, CLAUDE_CODE_NO_FLICKER=0, and some terminals, such as the one inside Obsidian) it does not dock however wide the window is, and the band above the prompt carries the buttons as a row, under one dim line: "pane docks only in fullscreen — run /tui fullscreen". Run /tui fullscreen (Claude Code restarts and resumes the session) and the pane docks; CLAUDE_CODE_NO_FLICKER=1 before starting Claude Code does it for every session. A fullscreen window narrower than about 144 columns gets the same row without the line. Since gt 0.21.4 the pane no longer takes the "placed" answer Claude Code gives at session start, before the window is measured, as proof that it is on screen: it checks again on the first draw and again whenever the window changes size. /gt:gt-help opens a Getting started tab for your role; the last install question ("Open the guide?") opens it.

Your own LOTR connections, and MCP over stdio (gt-lotr 0.5.0, gt 0.21.4)

Make your own connection: tell Claude "make a LOTR connection for Acme" (or run lotr template guide). Save the service's docs page as a text file; lotr template new acme --from-doc docs.txt fills in what it can see and marks the rest TODO. Fix the TODOs, run lotr template check acme, put the secret in the store (store:acme-api) yourself, then lotr add-http acme@personal --from-template acme. A local MCP server that speaks stdio: lotr add-stdio notes@personal --command "npx -y @example/notes-mcp" --identity me --env NOTES_API_KEY=store:notes-key. LOTR starts it when first needed, reuses it, and stops it when idle; its stderr is kept as a masked tail and never echoed, and secrets are passed by reference only.

Connecting almost any MCP service (gt-lotr 0.5.0)

Say "connect Linear". Claude looks for a template (lotr template list: nine shipped archetypes, the catalog, your own), refreshes the catalog if needed (lotr template update), and if nobody has written one yet runs the research run (/gt-lotr:gt-lotr-research): it reads the vendor's own documentation, chooses an archetype and writes a template plus a fact sheet that gives every fact its source page and state (unverified, self-verified, independently-verified). It never touches a credential. The execution run is lotr setup NAME [--id name@zone], safe to repeat:

  1. checks the template (nothing left to fill in) and, for a program, that npx, uvx or docker exists (with how to install it);
  2. shows the exact command a stdio template will run on your machine and waits for your yes (--yes);
  3. checks the credential is at the reference the template names; if not it prints lotr secret put NAME for your own terminal (hidden prompt; the value never passes through Claude). For a browser sign-in it hands you lotr setup NAME to run in your terminal; a server whose OAuth app you must create asks for the client id and secret as references;
  4. adds the connection, then tests it: status, find, and one read that needs no input. The result is in plain words; exit 0 done, 3 waiting for you, 1 failed.

Which archetype: lotr template guide ends with a decision table (hosted + sign-in, hosted + own app, hosted + key, npx, uvx, docker, binary, legacy SSE bridge, plain REST). The sse-bridge archetype keeps its token in ~/.mcp-auth, outside LOTR's store; check and setup say so. The catalog is the repository's lotr-templates/ folder. Since gt 0.21.4 it is included with gt-lotr (a byte-for-byte copy in the module's templates/catalog/, checked against its catalog.json hashes every time a template is read), so right after an install, with no network, lotr template list and lotr setup NAME find it. It is also released independently of gt, and lotr template update refreshes it: a fetched catalog with a higher catalog_version wins over the included one, a lower or equal one never replaces it, and your own templates win over both. update verifies hashes and the user-side checks (the catalog is signed and a fetched one must verify against the pinned key, lotr catalog check says "signed: verified against the pinned key"; the hashes alone come from the same place as the files, so they catch damage, not a compromised repository, and installing a fetched template runs its command after --yes, so read the command first) and never creates a connection (see SECURITY.md 9.2 for what that does and does not prove). Catalog 1 has sixteen templates, all MCP servers: linear, sentry, notion, atlassian, gitlab (hosted, browser sign-in); pagerduty, google-drive, slack, m365 (hosted, an OAuth app you create); github (hosted, a token); playwright, filesystem, git, fetch, sqlite, postgres (programs on your machine). With a gt persona set, lotr setup with no template and lotr template list name the ones that suit your role.

Catalog 3 templates: twilio, tailscale, Google (gt-lotr 0.5.1, gt 0.22.0)

lotr template list also shows twilio and tailscale (REST, a key by reference: reads are reads, everything that sends, calls, changes or deletes asks first), google-gmail, google-calendar, google-docs and google-sheets (Google's hosted servers with your own OAuth client, read-only scopes), and google-tasks (the Tasks REST API with an access token you store: lists and tasks are reads, adding or completing a task is a write, delete and clear ask first; the token is short-lived and not refreshed). lotr template facts-check FILE on a template's facts/NAME.facts.json shows where each fact came from and which are unverified (the tailscale paths and base URL, and the google-tasks header and token lifetime, are). None was called against a live service. A template holds no secret: credential_ref names an entry in your secrets store.

Connecting an MCP without knowing the answers (gt-lotr 0.5.1, gt 0.22.0)

lotr onboard NAME --url https://host/mcp (or --package 'npx -y pkg@1.2.3') works out which of the nine template archetypes fits, from what the server answers, in at most three requests. It sends one deliberately fake credential only to a key-style server (an OAuth server answers the same with or without one), never a real one, and it starts no package. It writes the template and a fact sheet from what it saw, leaves the rest as TODO and says so, and then runs lotr setup, which stops with the exact command for your own terminal when a key or a sign-in is needed. A server that fits no template says no_template_fits and writes nothing.

After the connection works: lotr onboard discover ID lists its operations, tiers and argument types into a manifest (no server description text is kept; a count of instruction-like sentences comes from a model with no tools); lotr onboard agent ID writes a reader agent, lotr-<name>-<zone>, that can run one script and nothing else (a gt guard enforces it; changes still go through the writer agents). The script returns a small result whole and a large one as a summary with the full result in a private file. Three module settings: onboard_direct_tokens (500, 1500 default, 3000, 6000, 12000; characters / 4, estimated), onboard_summarise (on default | off), onboard_summary_model (haiku default | sonnet | opus). lotr onboard compare ID shows the last stored results beside their summaries, and lotr onboard tune ID --direct-tokens N changes one connection's limit. Whether a summary keeps what you need is for you to judge there; the only check made so far is one sample (26,000 characters of JSON, every figure kept).

A plain MCP client using the hub (gt-lotr 0.5.0)

A hub (mode: hub) can serve MCP over HTTP at POST /mcp, for a client that is not Claude Code (any MCP-over-HTTP connector). It is off. In the hub's gateway.json set "listen": {"mcp_http": true}, lotr enroll the client (--max-tier write or lower unless the owner sits at the hub: a consent operation raises the hub's own confirmation), restart lotrd. The connector URL is http(s)://<hub>:<port>/mcp; it sends Authorization: Bearer <secret> and X-GT-Client: <id>. Test with curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $(cat <secret file>)" -H "X-GT-Client: assistant" -d '{"jsonrpc":"2.0","id":1,"method":"initialize","params":{"protocolVersion":"2025-06-18"}}' http://127.0.0.1:8765/mcp. A connector that cannot send headers needs "mcp_http_trust_loopback": true as well and the URL http://localhost:8765/mcp?cid=assistant; that works only from the hub machine, and every program there can then act as that client, so keep its tier low.

Help, hints and the LOTR changes (#9, #34, #35, #37, #38)

/gt:gt-help <command> plays any command's real output in a simulated terminal beside what it does, how it works, when to use it and what next (63 variants recorded against the demo vault); a Workflows tab holds 13 role workflows. next_step_hints and prompt_hints (on) add one Next: or Tip: line through systemMessage only, and hints_as_choices (on) also offers 2-3 next steps as a clickable question. gt-lotr 0.4.1 adds generic connection templates and agent_split (split or combined gateway agents).

Closing a session and completing things (BREAKING)

/gt:gt-complete now closes the session (the opposite of /gt:gt-open). Completing and archiving a project, task or handoff is /gt:gt-complete. There is no alias.

Other additions

/gt:gt-forget retracts a wrong fact (gt_forget.py); /gt:gt-grant lets one command through for a time (gt_grant.py); skeptic defaults (skeptic_pass on, skeptic_max_flags 7, skeptic_record_outcomes on; gt_skeptic.py); accept_review (dialog or full) for gt_accept.py; owner_command_script (off, or on to hand a needs_owner command over as a self-deleting script, zsh <path>, instead of the command on the clipboard) for gt_owner_cmd.py; owner_gate_presence (off, or on to let a Touch ID / Hello approval, with the whole small change in the dialog, stand in for the terminal on accept only: Touch ID only, at most 4 changed lines, never global-memory/, design.md or core-rules/, a daily cap; security setting, default off, see SECURITY.md 5.5c) for gt_accept.py; closeout_later_days for later --until <date>; ingest_specialists; knowledge_unread_days and knowledge_read_often (gt_use.py, read and use recency); savings_ledger. The lint has 26 checks, the newest being retraction-missing.

Where does this fact go?

The fact Goes to
"This preset is wrong right now" memory/
"This API rejects plural keys" research.md
"We use POST for all mutations, and why" decisions.md
"The system is structured like this now" design.md
"This platform behaviour bit two projects" Knowledge/
"Always diff before overwriting prod" global-memory/
"How to restart the prod backtest" runbook.md
"Never scp without backing up" PROTOCOL.md
"This code lives on host X at path Y" source.md
"Which box serves which role" INFRASTRUCTURE.md

Two failure modes, opposite directions: too narrow buries a platform truth in one project, so the next rediscovers it the hard way; too wide promotes a quirk into global-memory/, where it loads into unrelated sessions as a rule that isn't true there. When unsure, file narrow — promotion is cheap, demotion isn't.

/gt:gt-route applies this table for you, against the material actually in hand, without your having to hold the table in your head mid-session. Reach for it when the answer is not obvious — including when the honest answer is that the fact belongs to a project other than the one loaded.

Script reference

SCRIPTS=~/.claude/plugins/cache/golden-thread-plugin/gt/0.18.1/scripts

python3 $SCRIPTS/vault_init.py fresh --vault ~/my-vault --domain "My Team"

python3 $SCRIPTS/vault_init.py create-project --vault ~/my-vault \
  --name my-project --title "My Project" --tags "backend,api" \
  --domain platform --topology bastion-direct \
  --repo-url git@github.com:me/my-project.git --runbook

python3 $SCRIPTS/vault_init.py create-project --vault ~/my-vault \
  --name sub-feature --parent my-project --domain platform

python3 $SCRIPTS/vault_init.py connect --vault ~/existing-vault

python3 $SCRIPTS/vault_init.py install-core-rules --vault ~/my-vault

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_ingest.py ~/Projects/my-project --json

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_lint.py ~/my-vault --queue ~/my-vault/review-queue.md

python3 $SCRIPTS/gt_settings.py show

gt_tasks.py runs from the vault, at Projects/golden-thread/tools/, beside gt_adr.py, gt_closeout.py, gt_edits.py, gt_events.py, gt_log.py, gt_session.py, gt_spool.py and safe_write.py. All nine ship as templates and are seeded there by vault_init.py fresh or connect (what /gt:gt-init runs); install.sh refreshes copies that predate the installed template. It regenerates TASKS.md from every project's ## Tasks section.

python3 ~/my-vault/Projects/golden-thread/tools/gt_tasks.py

It infers the vault from its own location, so it needs no arguments; --vault is there to override that. Re-run it before reading TASKS.md — project priority is computed against the clock (stale-P1 ageing, deadline windows, pp_escalate), so the ranking changes with time even when no file has changed. TASKS.md is generated and must never be hand-edited.

--json prints the ranked rollup to stdout without writing TASKS.md. Deadline windows may span days (0.13.0): Fri 16:00 → Sun 16:44 America/Chicago -> 0, including spans that wrap the week; the zone always comes from the rule, never the machine.

gt_lint.py --json emits findings as JSON, and gt_lint.py --runbooks reports lines duplicated across projects' runbooks — the detection step of /gt:gt-runbook-lint.

Added in 0.18.1 — each is described where its activity is; this is the command line.
